## A.21 Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

Congo is committed to pursuing low-carbon emission-resilient development seeking to reduce its GHG emissions by 30% below business as usual in 2030 compared with the baseline of 48 MtCO2e in 2010 (67% from deforestation and 33% from energy), and by 55% by 2035. The country's GHG reduction strategy is principally based on its sustainable forest management effort to reduce deforestation, which accounts for about 81% of the total emissions. In addition, Congo has committed in its Congo’s NDC, carbon emissions from deforestation represent 81% of the country’s total GHG emissions. To reduce these emissions, Congo is implementing their national REDD+ strategy, followed by the REDD+ National Investment Framework (NIF) for the period 2015-2025.

Located at the heart of the Congo Basin forests, the Republic of Congo possesses a vast forest area estimated at 37 million hectares. The country’s total deforestation rate is estimated to be about 4% per year and its annual deforestation rate estimated at 8.00% (2015). Congo's forests are now subject to increasing anthropogenic pressure, mainly from slash-and-burn farming for agricultural expansion and illegal logging.

Slash-and-burn farming for agricultural expansion and illegal logging collection are the main intensified, direct drivers of deforestation in the country. The rate of deforestation is more pronounced in the south of the country where the majority of the population lives.

In addition to deforestation, Congo also faces challenges from its large rural population practicing subsistence farming as their principal economic activity, and more than 80% of the Congolese population depends on firewood as their main source of energy. The country’s agricultural sector is characterized by low productivity due to poor production technology leading to irreducibly productive and quality of crops such as cassava, maize, groundnuts and plants are also limited in terms of access to credit facilities, inputs, extension services, and other financial security rights which prevents a long term investment in low emission crop production. (ii) Limited access to a market for agricultural products, which results in low prices and lack of income generation, leading to low incomes and invest in low emission crop production; and (iv) Limited access to climate finance for green agriculture investments, which is a major constraint to the adoption of sustainable land management practices that help them shift away from unsustainable and high emission practices without the need for additional public funding.

Congo believes that this deforestation rate could double in the coming years, mainly due to pressure on the forests. This will have a significant impact on the country’s economy as well as its ability to meet its NDC commitments which would only mitigate by 2.7 t a year carbon scenario. Increased deforestation not only contributes to the country's rising GHG emissions but also leads to soil erosion, loss of biodiversity and other environmental degradation.

This project aims at reducing carbon emissions, while also providing important adoption co-benefits, focusing its action on three deforestation and forest degradation hotspots. These a agricultural and fuelwood supply basins are located in the provinces of Bas-Congo, Bandundu and Lualaba. The project will be implemented in the cities and towns of Dolisie, Madigou and Ndayi. They extend over the departments of Plateaux, Pool, Bouenza, Niari and Kwango. These areas represent 10% of Congo’s total forest area but are responsible for about 35% of deforestation during the period 2012-16.

Sustainable land management and unsustainable production and consumption of fuelwood were intrinsically linked and cannot be addressed in silos, as fuelwood is collected mostly from clearing more and more land for farming. The situation is not only a problem related to deforestation but also a major challenge to the country’s food security and agricultural productivity as a result of obsolete farming practices. Additionally, limited access to land and insecure land tenure services hinder agricultural practices associated with high GHG emissions, while also preventing the planting of

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2 | PAGE 3 OF 71

Trees. This is compounded by poor communities' restricted access to finance, which prevents them from adapting long-term land-use systems.

To address these critical issues, the project adopts a holistic approach, essentially to achieve transformational change at scale, and to ensure sustainability and proposes a series of interventions around three integrated coherent components: (i) the establishment of Multi-Party Forest Funds (MPFs), which will provide financial resources that lay the groundwork for other activities by focusing on the securing of land access for small producers, participatory mapping, and the development of forest management plans; (ii) the establishment of innovative financing mechanisms and systems and the establishing of approximately 13,000 ha of micro-and small-sized low emission, climate-resident agricultural land-use systems, which will provide a basis for the creation of Microfinance Institutions (MFIs), removing barriers faced by farmers in accessing micro-finance, enabling them to produce business plans for further development and access credit; and (iii) the establishment of an innovative financing mechanism that provides financial resources adapted to the needs of farmers.

This integrated approach will result in a paradigm shift for the poor communities by enabling them to transition away from a high-emission and unsustainable BAU scenario towards a long-term, sustainable low-carbon development pathway.

PREFOREST will build on FAO's extensive experience, both in the country and in the sector. Co-financing from FAO through resources provided by the Central Africa Forest Initiative (CAF), the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) and the Global Environment Facility (GEF) will ensure that the project has a significant impact. The project will also catalyze private sector investment in order to sustain and scale up interventions beyond the project period.

The project is aligned with the GCF's Strategic Objective 1: "To support countries in reducing greenhouse gas emissions by between the GCF, FAO, CAF and IFAD. It has the potential to significantly transform forestry and land-use practices on a large scale, and to provide a replicable model for other countries in sub-Saharan Africa, including those of the Congo Basin – by these key players – through a programmematic approach and in a coordinated manner.

Numerous stakeholders have been engaged throughout the project preparation phase. These include: various sectors of government; civil society organizations (CSOs); private sector representatives; local communities; and other actors such as the World Bank, GEF, UNDP, FAO, CIDA, USAID, WFP, IUCN, WWF, CIFOR, TNC, Rainforest Foundation US, Rainforest Action Network, Greenpeace, and others. The project will also contribute to reducing carbon emissions by 8.4 million tonnes of CO2 equivalent (equivalent CO2 eq) per year. 6.72 million tonnes of CO2 eq over the eight-year period of project implementation, or 18.73 million tonnes of CO2 eq over a 50-year horizon.

B.1 Climate rationale and context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

The Republic of Congo (Congo) is situated in Central Africa at the heart of the world's second largest contiguous forest area, covering an area of about 375,000 km² or 29% of its total land area, approximately 69.8% of the total land area. Within the context of its Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC), Congo has committed to reducing greenhouse gas emissions by at least 41% below business as usual (BAU) scenario by 2030.

This document provides an overview of the climate change mitigation strategy and actions that will contribute to a reduction of GHG emissions from deforestation and forest degradation and increased forest carbon stocks in the Congo. According to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), emissions from the forestry sector represent the second largest source of greenhouse gas emissions after energy production.

A business as usual (BAU) scenario suggests a sixfold increase in total CO2 emissions (approximately 43,527 Mt eq CO2) by 2035, while it would only multiply by 2.7 in a low-carbon scenario. To reduce its GHG emissions, the Congo has developed a REDD+ strategy, followed by the REDD+ National Investment Framework (NIF) for the period 2018-2025.

Direct drivers of deforestation and forest degradation: i) Slash-and-burn agriculture; ii) Industrial agriculture; iii) Fuelwood production; iv) Commercial logging/illegal logging; v) Mining development; vi) Road and urban infrastructure.

Indirect (or underlying) drivers of deforestation and forest degradation: i) weak governance due to weak institutional capacity, lack of political will, corruption, and poor coordination among institutions; ii) population growth, poverty, and lack of land-use planning, leading to overlapping and incompatible land uses; iii) poverty and lack of financing (or access to credit) for smallholder farmers; iv) low agricultural productivity and limited diversification of livelihoods lives below the national poverty line, and 60.5% lives in rural areas; vi) population growth of 2.9%, leading to an increase in domestic demand for agricultural land, fuelwood and infrastructure Congo's forests are indeed subject to deforestation and forest degradation due to these drivers, which have a direct impact on the livelihoods of local populations. Slash-and-burn farmers adapt at agriculture expansion and fuelwood collection is the main driver of deforestation and forest degradation.

Substitution of slash-and-burn farming by cash crops such as cocoa, coffee, palm oil, and rubber, which generates limited yields and soil erosion, is another major driver of deforestation and forest degradation. This substitution means represents approximately 84% of cultivated land and 98% of national food production (BRALI, 2014). Smallholder farmers engage in traditional agriculture mainly based on cassava and other crops such as maize, beans, peanuts, and vegetables. The slash-and-burn system is a form of shifting cultivation which allows the forest to replenish. Although slash and burn agriculture may be practiced sustainably under certain conditions, it has been shown that this type of farming is unsustainable in Congo due to its low productivity and unsuitable agricultural practices characterized by low productivity." are pushing smallholders farmers to practice slash-and-burn agriculture, which leads to deforestation and forest degradation. This is because the slash-and-burn system allows farmers to constantly push back the agricultural frontier to the detriment of the forest.

The impact of climate change on crop production in Congo is expected to be significant, with an acceleration being climate change (section 8.0 of this feasibility study). In southern Congo, the average temperatures have increased by 0.5°C per decade since 1960 and are projected to increase at a rate of about 2.5°C over the period 1960-2030. Projections from RCP4.5 emission scenario reveal an increase in temperatures of about 2.5°C over the period 1960-2030, with a further increase to approximately 7°C by 2080-2100. The average rainfall is projected to decrease by at least 30% from 2020 to 2050.

The direct impacts of rainfall variability on crop production include early sowing (due to the advance of the rainy season), increased evaporation and transpiration, reduced soil moisture content (root system); and increased risk of flooding which has a considerable impact on agricultural systems. The result is a decrease in yields for major crops such as maize, beans, peanuts, and vegetables. This will have an adverse effect for low-income smallholder farmers. FAO's Climate Adaptation at Rural Development - Assessment Tool (CARDAT) indicates that the average yield of cassava could be reduced by 20% due to climate change and deforestation. The impact of climate change on crop production in Congo is expected to be significant, with an acceleration being climate change. Cassava production is predicted to decrease by approximately 20%, peanuts by 16%, and maize by 9%. Between 50-70% of the population depends directly or indirectly on agriculture for their livelihood.

Slash and burn fuelwood collection: Most of the fuelwood collected is collected from fallow land cleared for slash-and-burn agriculture (Louvigne, 2017). Approximately 4/5 of fuelwood is collected from natural forests and only 1/5 from plantations. The main species used are Eucalyptus globulus, Acacia mangium, Pinus radiata, and Casuarina equisetifolia as a source of energy for 90% of households." According to the Second National Communication (NDC) submitted by Congo on September 21st, 2016, the country aims to reduce national emissions by 34% below business as usual levels by 2030. To achieve its emission reduction targets, the Republic of Congo has set two areas of focus: (i) Maintain, or even increase, forest cover and carbon stocks through sustainable forest management practices and reforestation; (ii) Reduce GHG emissions from the energy sector and the fight against unplanned deforestation.

The most important business as usual (BAU) barriers that are perpetuating deforestation and forest degradation through slash and burn agriculture include:

(i) limited access to improved crop production technology to intensify productivity and quality of crops such as cassava, maize, groundnuts and plantains and to increase soil fertility in order to reduce the need to clear forests for new land;

(ii) limited land access and security rights which prevents a long term investment in low emission crop production;

(iii) limited access to a sustainable source of fuelwood which would induce pressure on natural forest;

(iv) limited access to credit facilities from financial institutions which prevents smallholders from accessing finance for green agroforestry investments which prevents smallholders from unsustainable and high-emission practices without the need for additional public finance.

In order to reduce this trend and improve the livelihoods of the smallholders farmers in Southern Congo, the following needs have been identified in the project area:

(i) Regenerating land after slash and burn agriculture and increase soil fertility and crop productivity for low emission and sustainable crop production by introducing innovative agro-forestry practices;

(ii) Reduce pressure on existing natural forests by planting trees for sustainable fuelwood production in areas where there is no access to a sustainable source of fuelwood.

(iii) Strengthening land access and land security to allow for pilot delineation and pilot investment in order to reduce the need to clear new land for agriculture;

(iv) Strengthening connection between smallholder farmers and markets, as well as strengthening business skills among smallholders to increase their income and invest in high-quality and low emission crop production from agro-forestry.

(v) Breeding of improved varieties of crops in order to increase investment and scaling up of high quality and low emission crop production from agro-forestry.


Government integrated approach to reduce emissions in the forestry and land use sector:

Congo ratified the Paris Climate Agreement on April 21st, 2017, having previously submitted its Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) on September 21st, 2016. The NDC submitted at COP23 in Paris aims to reduce national emissions by 34% below business as usual levels by 2030.

To achieve its emission reduction targets, the Republic of Congo has set two areas of focus: (i) Maintain, or even increase, forest cover and carbon stocks through sustainable forest management practices and reforestation; (ii) Reduce GHG emissions from the energy sector and the fight against unplanned deforestation.


---
**Page 11**
---

In order to tackle the deforestation problem Congo has developed important elements of the Warsaw Framework for REDD+:
- the submission of its Forest Reference Emission Levels (FREL) in UNFCCC;
- the adoption of a REDD+ Investment Plan (REDD+ IP);
- the development of a REDD+ Investment Plan (REDD+ IP), which together form concrete instruments to guide its REDD+ investments.

The REDD+ IP, validated in June 2016, constitutes the reference framework for actions to be implemented during the period 2018–2023. The REDD+ IP is structured around a programmatic approach by identifying 5 enabling programs and 6 progressively integrated programs for the 2018-2023 period to be financed through existing bilateral and multilateral funding (see feasibility studies). These include: the Congo Basin Forest Partnership (CBFP), the Central African Forest Initiative (CAFI) and the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and other multilateral, bilateral donors as well as private sector actors.

PREFOREST has been formulated to be perfectly aligned with the REDD+ IP priority programs in coordination with other investments (CAF). World Bank is order to support Congo implementing its programmatic approach for emissions reduction and access carbon markets.

The main actors involved in this programmatic approach to achieve NDCs targets through REDD+ are:
- CAFI: CAFI is a member of the Central African Forest Initiative (CAFI) co-funding initiative created by several donor countries. The CAFI has been supporting Congo since 2015, and has provided USD 3 million to support the development of its REDD+ IP and to implement a Letter of Intent (LoI) signed on 3 September 2019 with CAFI donor countries. This LoI defines concrete actions for the implementation of the REDD+ IP in the framework of the CAFI, including: supporting Congo to invest an initial amount of USD 65 million to implement the REDD+ IP; with fund mobilization being based on a combination of public and private sector funding. The CAFI has also provided USD 10 million for the development of energy access (see table I). CAFI and FAO have been collaborating in Congo since 2015 and have agreed to support the country’s efforts to reduce deforestation and forest degradation, as well as to improve rural communities’ access to sustainable fuelwood. The combined impact of these interventions will multiply the individual results achieved by each intervention. In this regard, CAFI has agreed to provide resources to the amount of USD 7 million, to be channeled through FAO, for the implementation of the energy access component of the REDD+ IP in complementarity and alignment with the CAFI results framework. The main objectives, both are expected to contribute to climate change mitigation by reducing deforestation and forest degradation, as well as to improve rural communities’ access to sustainable fuelwood. The combined impact of these interventions will multiply the individual results achieved by each intervention.
- GCF: The GCF has provided USD 10 million for the development of energy access in Congo. It is expected to be mobilized in the long term (2025 onwards) to support CAFI and Congo’s efforts to reduce deforestation and forest degradation, as well as to improve rural communities’ access to sustainable fuelwood. The project represents the GCF-CAF collaboration to fight climate change.
- World Bank: The World Bank has provided USD 18 million through the EBR Program for Rural Electrification in Congo (Sangha and Likouala Departments), for an estimated amount of 60M USD. The ER-Program presents an opportunity for Congo to improve rural electrification, while contributing to climate change mitigation by reducing deforestation and forest degradation, as well as improving access to sustainable fuelwood. The World Bank has also provided USD 18 million through the Program for Community Agroforestry and Wood Energy Project (PACBE) are already secured and likely be to established in 2021.
- Government (Ministry of Forest Economy): The Government started promoting agroforestry systems through the implementation of the PACBE project. This project is expected to contribute to climate change mitigation by reducing deforestation and forest degradation, as well as improving access to sustainable fuelwood to supply the Brazzaville market. As an example, PROCARB has signed in 2019 a partnership with the Société des plantations Forestières Étatiques (SPFE), for the establishment of 30,000 ha of plantation of

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 21 | PAGE 8 OF 73

**Piloteuse Bakaté, Bamboo Mignon area. The Government also promoted community-based forest management such as the Makaka Project to address the sustainable supply of fuelwood.**

*FAO; the PREFOREST builds on FAO's extensive experience in agroforestry and sustainable supplies of woody biomass for energy and other uses. It aims at reducing deforestation from agricultural expansion and fuelwood collection in order to achieve REDD+ targets.*

**In order to increase the overall impact of PREFOREST, it is proposed to integrate the Project on Agro-Energy (PALE) project led by International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD). In order to increase the overall impact of PALE, it is proposed to integrate the PREFOREST project.**

*The PALE project in Congo estimated at USD 23 million.* The Development Objective of PALE is to reduce poverty and hunger through improved access to markets and increased productivity of smallholder farmers in the agricultural sector. It will be implemented by IFAD and FAO, with a strong focus on gender equality. The PREFOREST project will provide high support by facilitating access to tailored (appropriated/adapted) and targeted financial services for the first time to many small-scale producers. This will be achieved through the development of an innovative financing mechanism based on the access of 4,000 small-scale producers and their organizations to markets by means of long-term, equitable financial services.

Unlike CAFI interventions, which seek to reduce GHG emissions from the forest sector; PALE is a development project with no GHG emission reduction targets. Nevertheless, there are potential synergies/complementarities between PREFOREST and PALE. The integration of the two projects will increase the impact of PREFOREST under Component 3 by strengthening and scaling up smallholder farmers' access to markets.

An overview of these and other projects in the sector is provided in the table below:

## B.2.3.1.2. Key challenges (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagram)

This Theory of Change (ToC) seeks to address the main driver of deforestation and forest degradation in Congo: The expansion of agriculture into forests, which is driven by poverty, lack of access to land and credit, and unsustainable fuelwood production in southern Congo, towards low-emission, climate-resilient development pathways.

Current slash-and-burn farming caused by agriculture expansion and the consequent unsustainable production of food crops and fuel wood has led to deforestation and forest degradation. This is further exacerbated by a lack of tenure security for agricultural land, which is compounded by very low agricultural productivity due to obsolete farming practices, increased demand of crops and fuelwood, and limited access to credit and markets. The expansion of agriculture into forests also leads to the loss of agricultural practices associated with high GHG emissions, and discourages long-term investment and planting of trees.

PREFOREST proposes a holistic approach to sustainably address the main driver of deforestation in southern Congo: slash-and-burn farming. This ToC is based on the underlying theory that (i) tenure access and tenure rights are second; (ii) land use change from agriculture to agroforestry and fuelwood production is third, and (iii) the demand for agroforestry intensification is fourth, then deforestation and forest degradation associated GHG emissions from forests will be reduced, and finally, the demand for agricultural products will be met with a more sustainable and ultimately harmonized.

### Strengthening tenure access and security rights
The current practice of "temporary leases" limited to one or two years is not sufficient to reduce subsistence crop-cycle frequency short-cycle agricultural practices and impedes the investment required to stabilize slash-and-burn farming. The project will work with communities, local governments, and other stakeholders to strengthen land tenure security by reducing the pressure on natural forests. The project proposes three support options to improve access of small farmers to land: (i) strengthening community-based forest management institutions; (ii) improving tenure rights for agricultural land through communication about the opportunities and advantages of agroforestry and fuelwood plantations, and securing tenure for these activities.

### Deployment of low-emission climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry systems
The knowledge of climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry systems is limited in Congo. Some farmers claimed that they had never practiced or been trained in agroforestry, though they were interested to learn more with a view to diversifying their livelihoods. The project will work with communities and local governments to disseminate technologies not only increases the diversity of agricultural work, but also significantly limits productivity, often resulting in lower yields than monoculture crops. This is because farmers are not aware of the opportunities and advantages through communication about the opportunities and advantages of agroforestry and fuelwood plantations and securing tenure for these activities.

### As an immediate measure to reduce forest degradation
Existing operations will be built on to provide fuelwood through plantations of fast-growing species. A major project innovation for the Congo will be the introduction of the


---
**Page 14**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL2.1 | PAGE 10 OF 73

#### Facilitating access to markets, strengthening national agricultural financing structures, and business capacities – Farmers and producers have little information on market opportunities and quality requirements, which constrains their commercialization of agroforestry products. The project will address this by developing a marketing plan and packaging requirements for higher value products. Through IFAD co-financing, the project will build capacity on technical aspects such as post-harvest handling and storage, and provide training to farmers and producers of agroforestry goods supported by the project with markets through the development and implementation of a marketing plan. The project will also support the creation of a national network of market facilitators (see Letters of Intent from WFP, Eco-DE, SCIDE, EPPAWA, AGRODEC, Zando Market, Cluster Union pour l'Agroforesterie et la Biodiversité, and others). This network will be responsible for providing technical assistance to cover the yearly production of most of the crops produced under the agroforestry systems established by project beneficiaries. The network will also provide market information on prices and demand.

The financial sector in Congo is still evolving with the agriculture sector representing only 6% of the total portfolio in commercial banks. Most farmers do not have access to credit from formal financial institutions (FFIs). The majority of credit applications by farmers do not meet the minimum requirements for secured loans, which are usually provided at high interest rates and short maturity terms. Farmers also face insecure tenure rights (MFI don't finance such farms) and the informal and embryonic nature of the value chains for agroforestry products. In addition, most MFIs in Congo offer only a limited range of financial services to farmers offered (i.e. high interest rate about 4-5% per month and short-term maturity) and lack of technical skills to assess creditworthiness of farmers. The project will support the development of a national network of microfinance credit practices, which are widespread in all departments, notably fortunes or rioting savings and credit associations, and provide training on financial literacy and business management for farmers. It will also support the creation of money collection services on local markets (mokidissia). This makes it difficult to invest in sustainable low external input systems.

Project activities to secure small farmer tenure rights will be fundamental to improving their access to credit. In addition, the development of business plans and the creation of strategic partnerships with MFIs with an interest in financing the agroforestry sector will help farmers to improve their access to credit. The project will also support microfinance credit in line with harvest cycles with low interest rates and longer maturity periods. Increased access to micromortgage loans for smallholder farmers will allow them to invest in agroforestry systems, including enhancing entrepreneurial skills to sustain their activities.

### Notes
* Named after a successful project in the Democratic Republic of Congo which introduced an improved fast-growth agroforestry practice.

### B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2000 words, approximately 4 pages)

The PREFOREST project aims to reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation caused by slash-and-burn agriculture aimed at the interlinked issues of agriculture expansion and unsustainable production of food/feed crops. The project seeks to achieve this through a combination of technical assistance and financial support for transformational change at scale, thus optimizing impact in terms of emission reductions, and ensuring long-term sustainability. The approach includes the following: (1) Strengthening of land access and security rights for long term


---
**Page 16**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 2.1 | PAGE 12 OF 73

### agro-intensity investments: (i) Deployment and stabilization of low-carbon agriculture and forestry systems to increase production, increase soil fertility and regenerate and reduce pressure on natural forests; and (ii) Strengthening of national capacity for REDD+ implementation.

The Project fits into Congo's overall efforts to reduce GHG emissions from land use and land use change and deforestation (LULUCF) and climate change vulnerability under its NDC. In particular, the project is aligned with the National REDD+ Strategy (NRS) of the Republic of Congo (ROC), which aims at reducing deforestation and forest degradation while increasing agricultural productivity as part of the priorities to reduce emissions from deforestation and land degradation in Congo.

The Project will focus its action on large agricultural and fuelwood production basins in the Republic of Congo, which have been identified by the Ministry of Forest Economy and Environment (MFE) as priority areas for REDD+ activities. The majority of the Congolese population resides. PREFOREST will build on successful experiences within the Congo and neighboring countries such as DRC, Gabon, and Equatorial Guinea in developing and implementing agroforestry systems and agricultural productivity as part of the priorities to reduce emissions from deforestation and land degradation in Congo.

The Project area is located in the northwestern part of the country. The project will focus on three large basins: the North Congo and the Mampu agroforestry system developed in DRC which contributed to agriculture sedimentation and soil fertility, as well as the production of fuelwood in a way that preserved forest cover; the basin of the Sangha-Tsikata River, which is characterized by high deforestation rates (133,000 tons of CO2 per year over an area of 7,500 ha), as well as the production of fuelwood in a way that preserved forest cover; and the basin of the Ogooué River, which has been identified as a priority for REDD+ by development partners such as IFAD, CAFI and the Ministry of Forest Economy as co-finances. WFP, ECOF, CONGO-REDD, and other actors are already implementing projects in this area.

Moreover, the PREFOREST project has been designed to complement more than fifteen ongoing related initiatives, 20 which will be described below with a brief analysis/whole impact.

### Justification of the project site selection

The Project will concentrate its action on three large agricultural product and fuelwood supply basins in the country. These basins are located in the northwestern part of Congo, specifically in the departments of Bas-Congo and Pointe-Noire, which will be the subject of actions to stimulate the adoption of improved stoves. Data is consistent with the ones used for the third national communication under development by Congo.

All 13 identified districts experienced an increase in forest cover loss (deforestation and degradation combined)**[1]** between the two periods of comparison (2000-2012 and 2012-2016), notably with respect to forest degradation. The most affected areas were located in the southern part of the country (Pointe-Noire, Dolisie, Brazzaville) and the northern towns with agricultural, forestry and fuelwood products. The area under pressure was also the Sangha-Mbaere National Park and the Bouenza Forest Reserve. The forests under pressure were largely affected by deforestation during the period 2000-2012, and by degradation during the period 2012–2016**[2]** (see Figure 5).

### Output 1.2: Land access and security rights of beneficiaries strengthened

It is firmly established – including in Congo – that any robust response to deforestation and forest degradation relies heavily on both land tenure access and security of rights.* Field visits in the project areas revealed that access rights to land are often limited, especially for smallholders. The current legal framework governing land use in Congo has evolved significantly over the last decade, and access and security rights still have many limitations, especially for smallholders. In general, there is a lack of clarity on the tenure status of land (whether it is state-owned or private), (formal land ownership or customary land ownership) and small producers, on private land, which are limited to one or two hectares. The current legal framework also does not provide any specific rights for forest users, which discourages the planting of trees. Under such conditions where the access and security rights are not guaranteed; it is difficult to implement interventions that aim at reducing deforestation and forest degradation.

Three support options are considered by the project, and the relevance of each will depend on the local context of each option. These three options are: (i) land tenure regularization through the PRORAN programme (Programme National d'Arborisation et de Réorestation – PRORAN) or land available on former State farms; free of charge to beneficiaries for climate-resilient agroforestry or forest-based activities, in exchange for a commitment by part of land beneficiaries for climate-resilient agroforestry or forestry activities in exchange for the project’s support. (ii) Land tenure regularization through the PRORAN programme or land available on former State farms; free of charge to beneficiaries for climate-resilient agroforestry or forest-based activities, in exchange for a commitment by part of land beneficiaries for climate-resilient agroforestry or forestry activities in exchange for the project’s support. (iii) Land tenure regularization through the PRORAN programme or land available on former State farms; free of charge to beneficiaries for climate-resilient agroforestry or forest-based activities, in exchange for a commitment by part of land beneficiaries for climate-resilient agroforestry or forestry activities in exchange for the project’s support.

** Selection criteria for pre-selected beneficiaries: (i) Willingness to commit to making land available to beneficiaries interested in implementing climate-resilient agroforestry or forest-based activities; (ii) Willingness to commit to a change of land use (please refer to Section 2.6 of the feasibility study for more detail on each option).

Activity 1.2.2 Development of formal agreements with beneficiaries and landowners and provision of support to strengthen tenure security rights

Then, the Project will provide legal and administrative support for the development, signature and formalization of agreements between the Project and individual smallholder farmers and forest communities as well as with landowners. Transfer of user rights will be subject to compliance to a list of specifications, including the obligation to comply with the terms of the agreement and the obligations set out in the Forest Management Plan by PRONAR. In respect to tenure security rights, the Project will primarily support departmental directors of land administration and forest management units in the implementation of the procedures for issuing certificates of title and licenses. The procedures for transferring land access and rights to beneficiaries will be defined within the framework of the project’s strategy for addressing gender inequality and social inclusion, with a focus on the most marginalized (e.g., women, indigenous people). In this regard, the Project will seek to secure access and tenure rights over a total area of at least 3,000 ha for women and marginalized groups, notably indigenous people.

Component 2: Establishment of agroforestry and forestry systems for climate change mitigation

Component 2 consists of supporting initiatives, implemented by individual smallholder farmers and their communities in the form of agroforestry and/or forest plantations. These initiatives will contribute to reducing soil erosion, further deforestation for agricultural extension through increased soil fertility and productivity and provide a sustainable source of income for local populations. They will also help to mitigate climate change by sequestering carbon dioxide (CO2) degradation, increase smallholder farmers’ adaptive capacity, and reduce their vulnerability to climate change. The initiatives will be implemented in the same areas as those identified under Component 1, with the exception of the environment developed under Component 1. The activities carried out by the beneficiaries of this component will be eligible for co-financing through CAF resources (see section III of the feasibility study for more information).

The component will follow an integrated landscape approach (ILA) framed around multifunctionality of the forest ecosystem and driven by participation and cross-sectoral processes. In this approach, individual farms will be managed as part of a larger landscape that includes other land uses such as agriculture, livestock production, and water resources management. This will contribute to sustainable wood energy availability. It will also contribute to biodiversity conservation as well as improved well-being for the local population (see section III of the feasibility study for more information).

Objective 2: Fast start forestry systems for energy purposes established (FAO co-financing through CAF resources)

Activity 2.1.1 Provision of technical assistance* for the establishment of fast start forestry systems for energy purposes

Through resources provided by CAF and executed by FAO, this output seeks to establish approximately 2,700 ha of fast-start agroforestry and/or forest plantations in the project area. These will be established as a fuelwood supply source from the early stages of project intervention in order to avoid tree cutting of natural forests for firewood purposes. The establishment of these fast start systems will be carried out through the planting of fast growing species such as acacia* that take at least 7 years. Forestry systems can be carried out on degraded land, abandoned agricultural land or other areas where there is a need to rehabilitate the soil and the potential to be scaled up. Potential partners have been identified during the formulation phase.* The information will be aligned with what highlighted in the business plans.

It should be noted that the establishment of these fast start systems will be implemented with contributions from beneficiaries through the provision of technical assistance by FAO. This contribution is expected to be provided under PREFOST to sustain the commemorative act of 2,700 ha for the PREFOST project. The objectives may be updated according to the needs and priorities of the communities involved in the project.

* Acacia is already widely utilized within PRONAR (Ministry of Forest Economy), including for previous projects such as the one implemented by FAO in 2016, which planted 200 ha Acacia plantations on its 10,000ha of secured land in the Plateau-Bakola in 2016 for the production of fuelwood.

**Activity 2.2.1 will be carried out in two phases:**

## Output 2.2: Low emission, climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry systems established

### Activity 2.2.1 Awareness raising on climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry systems established

A communication (information and awareness) strategy targeting smallholder farmers as well as policy makers will be developed to raise awareness of the benefits of agroforestry and forest systems, including information about local opportunities and benefits with respect to agroforestry and forestry systems. The communication strategy will be implemented through a combination of mass media campaigns, community meetings, and other outreach activities with a strong emphasis on the direct participation of local communities.

### Activity 2.2.2 Provision of technical assistance for establishment to smallholder farmers/producers (MoF co-financing)

The project will support the Ministry of Finance in drafting administrative and legal modalities to transfer land from PRINOR and other land available on former State farms to beneficiaries for the implementation of agroforestry systems. The project will also provide technical assistance to beneficiaries with respect to the procedures required under the transfer of user rights. Meetings will be organized with relevant government secretaries and local authorities in relation to the acquisition of land and use, including forest lands.

### Activity 2.2.3 Organization of training for climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry systems

Selected beneficiaries will be trained on innovative/proven and totally relevant low emission, climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry systems that are appropriate to their local conditions. Training will focus on how smallholder farmers can use the appropriate combinations of ground and tree crops and the ways in which certain tree crops can enrich the soil and improve the environment for other crops.

Training will be provided using a participatory approach, adapted to the needs and constraints of each group will be developed and implemented. Beneficiaries will be organized into groups based on their level of education, age, gender, literacy, gender, social proximity, etc. A specific training format will be developed for the training of trainers.

Key elements of the FFS approach include:

- (i) Targeted smallholder farmers;
- (ii) Training is in groups (e.g., diverse in terms of age, gender, experience, etc.);
- (iii) Education and capacity building are integrated into the process;
- (iv) Local and outside knowledge are integrated through observation, critical analysis, sharing and debate;
- (v) Continuous learning is promoted by developing approaches to improve decision-making skills;
- (vi) Learning is a continuous process – regular meetings are held at critical crop production stages.

## Activity 2.2.4: Provision of technical assistance for the establishment of agroforestry and forestry systems

Establishment of plantations will be implemented by FAO through procurement of goods and services.
The FFS approach will be used in the implementation of activity 2.2.1, while the training approach described in the activity 2.2.4 applies.

The FFS is an approach based on people-centered learning. It offers space for hands-on group learning to shift towards more sustainable practices. The approach is designed to build capacity and skills among smallholder farmers through a participatory process that encourages them to share their knowledge, experience, and ideas in order to improve production and livelihoods. Upscaling is achieved through training of trainers approach to FFS. More information available [here](#).

The project will support beneficiaries in establishing approximately 11,800 ha of micro and small-sized forest ecosystems (5-50 ha on average)**[2]** in the 13 target districts. By establishing these forests, the project aims to: a) reduce pressure on natural forests; b) lead to GHG emission reductions from slash-and-burn agriculture for agroforestry systems; c) increase soil organic carbon and nitrogen content through the use of leguminous trees; d) provide additional income opportunities for smallholder farmers. Different agro-forestry models have been analysed by FAO and CIRAD**[3]** and the models selected for project implementation are those that have already been tested in the field, such as the model implemented in Congo, increasing the impact potential in terms of climate change mitigation with additionality co-benefits (please refer to Annex 2.1.4 of the economic feasibility study) and activity 2.2.4 a of the Feasibility Study: Feasibility study for detailed information.

Innovative, proven,**[4]** and locally relevant production systems will be developed, depending on the type of vegetation in each zone, with the aim of increasing productivity while reducing pressure on natural forests by integrating tree species for the production of charcoal and fruit trees with substrates crops (e.g. cassava). The smallholder farmers will need to comply with the environmental and social safeguards established in advance**, which are contained in agreements signed between the project and the beneficiaries. In alignment with the concept of the approach, the project will ensure that the investment for smallholders' groups or individuals through technical assistance and procurement of different inputs (e.g., seeds, fertilizers) is not more than 10% of the total investment. **In case of the Congolese poplar** (the project will cover 100% of the investment. Financial viability will be ensured by the project through a combination of technical assistance and procurement of different inputs for project investment. Once farmers are convinced of the results of the system, it is assumed that sustainability will be ensured.

### Activity 2.2.5 Monitoring and evaluation of overall Project interventions

The Project will conduct a performance evaluation. An M&E officer will be contracted to carry out overall monitoring throughout the Project lifetime, but independent evaluators will be contracted for the interim and final evaluations.

## Output 2.3. Nurestions are rehabilitated and forests are restored

The project will support the rehabilitation of nurseries and adjoining infrastructures in Ngondjou (Koulou), Dolisie (Nari), Loudima (Bouina), and Kirele (Poto) to scale up seeding and sapling production potential in order to provide a sustainable supply of seedlings for reforestation. The project will also support the rehabilitation of existing nurseries, which are located at the sites where the project will support the implementation of assisted natural regeneration activities** in areas already degraded by land use change or forest conversion. These nurseries have been identified as being suitable to produce seedlings for forest transition areas. More specifically, the activity will target community land subject to strong anthropic pressure due to its proximity to human settlements and potential for agricultural production.

### Activity 2.3.1 Rehabilitation of nurseries (M€ co-financing)

Government managed nurseries within SNRs (Service Nationaux de Reboisement) premises will be restored and equipped with the necessary infrastructure, including seedbeds, greenhouses, and other facilities required for rehabilitation of existing nurseries (please refer to section F.2 of the Feasibility study for more information about the feasibility of each nursery). Based on the results from the needs assessment, a first draft rehabilitation plan will be developed for the forests identified.

The project will also support women's and other smallholder farmer groups to establish and/or manage tree nurseries in their communities or on their farms. This activity is expected to contribute to:

- **Activity 2.3.2 Deployment of Assisted Natural Regeneration**

The target area for the assisted natural regeneration mission is 5,000 ha. Assisted Natural Regeneration (ANR) will be carried out by smallholder farmers and women's groups in order to regenerate the natural forests and contribute to carbon sequestration. Criteria for selecting degraded forest areas include: (i) The level of degradation; (ii) The potential for regeneration; (iii) The availability of seedlings; and (iv) The proximity of the site to the location of the areas within the Project boundaries. (v) The potential to synergize with ongoing or future

## Output 2.4: Support for the upscaling of climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry models is provided

### Activity 2.4.1: Support for the upscaling of climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry models

The project will support the improvement and dissemination of selected agroforestry and forestry production systems, including those developed by the CGIAR Research Program on Forests, Trees and Agroforestry (FTA) and by the IRFA and ERCI procured parties in close collaboration with other relevant institutions, such as Lychee Agroforestry Development Center (LADC). The project will also support the development of formal partnerships professional de Loudima. Formal partnerships will be established with the objective of achieving actions aimed at upscaling the selected agroforestry and forestry models. The project will provide a framework for partnerships with each institution will stipulate roles and responsibilities from each party, including monitoring indicators and performance improvement in the agroforestry systems.

The project will therefore promote the production systems implemented by beneficiaries, measure their productivity as well as efficacy in reducing GHG emissions and creating multiple ecosystem services, and will support the development of new credit products. The project will also provide technical assistance to research institutions based on existing identified models, then approved by a scientific committee established by the CGIAR Research Program on Forests, Trees and Agroforestry (FTA). In addition, specific techniques will be adopted consistent with the methodological approach. Data will be collected, analyzed and disseminated through workshops, seminars, conferences, publications and reports, as well as eventually published in peer review journals. The local community will be the main beneficiary of the interventions.

## Component 3: Strengthening agroforestry financing structures, business capacities and value chains

Component 3 aims to support the growth of resilient community-based, low carbon agriculture and forestry entrepreneurship in Congo by strengthening access to rural credit by beneficiaries and by developing business capacity building for financial institutions. The project will also support the development of new credit products and supply sides. On the demand side, the Project will support the development of market-oriented agroforestry systems that are economically viable and sustainable over time. This will be achieved through a series of activities aimed at strengthening the value chain.

The Project will support the capacity building of MFN loan officers in climate finance and the development of new credit products for agroforestry systems. The project will also support intermediation between farmers and MFIs for the effective used of new credit products/services.

## Output 3.1: Beneficiaries of low carbon, climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry systems are supported in developing business plans

The Project will support beneficiaries who implement low-emission agroforestry and forestry systems in developing business plans that include financial management and marketing aspects. The selection criteria for the beneficiaries is selected based on their climate change mitigation impact potential* and according to the sustainability of operations one year after implementation. The project will also provide technical assistance to beneficiaries during the setting up of the Project and will include mentoring and training beneficiaries on financial and accounting aspects, effective management of operations, as well as processing and marketing.

* Forest areas to be regreened will not be considered as also separated from other landscape types. They have to be closely linked to the existing forest cover.
* The criteria for the business plans includes beneficiaries commitment to engage in non slash-and-burn agricultural practices.

## 1.2. Development and implementation of business plans in forest areas

The business plans are an essential tool to guarantee sustainability of the interventions and will be used for the development and implementation of the agroforestry systems. They will be developed during phase 3 (1.5) after the initial phase of project support. These business plans will also emphasize the whole products value chain they can produce, while maximizing overall PREFOREST impacts. The approach taken in this regard will be similar to that described above for all of these activities are smallholder farmers, who will benefit from new skills and knowledge in respect of the development and implementation of business plans.

The use of Rural Invest - a free FAO software – will be promoted for the development of business plans. Particular emphasis will be placed on building women and youth organizations' capacities with regard to business plan development.

Selected beneficiaries will be supported in the development and implementation of business plans. Each beneficiary will be responsible for developing one business plan, which will cover a minimum of 50% of his/her business plan, and each member may support several beneficiaries at the same time (between 5-10 beneficiaries). The beneficiaries should have some knowledge or experience with agroforestry management in the agriculture and forestry sectors, as well as having some knowledge of the local environment.

## Activity 3.2.1: Capacity building of national financial institutions on rural finance for agriculture (IFAD co-financing)

With IFAD-PAJE (Projet Agriculture, Jeunes et Entrepreneuriat - Agriculture, youth and entrepreneurship project) co-financing, the Project will build the capacity of selected MFIs in rural finance for agriculture as per PAJE priorities. The capacity building will be based on a set of key related policies and procedures; (ii) strengthening of the approval process of loans and monitoring and reporting systems; (iii) development of appropriate tools for the recording and monitoring of new financial products to be developed by MFIs in rural finance for agriculture.

A methodology guide will also be developed on rural finance for agriculture and this guide will be used as a basis of training for the selected MFIs. The training will be conducted over 2 days with IFAD technical support. Technical support will be provided to the selected MFIs on a regular basis so as to mainstream rural finance for agriculture development in their operations.

## Activity 3.2.2: Capacity building of national financial institutions on green investment in agroforestry and forestry

A capacity needs assessment will be carried out with respect to financial products and services that best fit the needs of beneficiaries. Local MFIs will be assisted to develop responsible green investment criteria specific to the local context, which will be used as a basis for the development of new financial products. This will support understanding and manage associated risks. Loan officers, who will work directly with the beneficiaries and provide them with technical assistance on the use of the funds, will receive training in this regard.

The cost of the Project outputs (e.g., green investment criteria, micro-credit products) into MFIs' financial operations will be covered by IFAD co-financing. Training for banks and microfinance institutions will include information on the particular constraints faced by women in agroforestry and forestry projects as well as raising awareness on this issue. There will also be efforts to tailor financial products to women's needs.

External evaluations of the MFIs that have received training will be conducted on a regular basis in order to identify any gaps or weaknesses in the implementation of the training, and to ensure that the training is specifically into how the knowledge from the training is being integrated into the regular operational finances of the MFI.

## Activity 3.2.3: Development of a national financial inclusion strategy and formalization of MFIs (IFAD co-financing)

The project will support the development of a national financial inclusion strategy with special emphasis on agroforestry and forestry financing. The main beneficiaries of this intervention - and of the project – are smallholder farmers who will benefit from new financial products and services, as well as MFIs, whose capacity


---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 2.1 | PAGE 24 OF 73

will be strengthened to widen their options to the agro-finance and forestry sub-sectors. Particular attention will be given in gender and women's access to financial institutions.

Under IFAD co-financing, a methodological approach for the development of a national financial inclusion strategy will be elaborated on the basis of the analysis of the current situation and needs identified through widespread consultation. The strategy will then be elaborated based on the approved methodology and the results from the stakeholder engagement process. This strategy will provide guidance to IFAD and other donors for operations in the agriculture and forestry sectors. These MFIs, will eventually serve as reference financial institutions for the development of microfinance products and services for agroforestry value chains promoted by PREFOREST under component 2. A mapping analysis will be conducted to identify relevant gaps with respect to this process. The project will then provide support, which includes information on the design and implementation of MFIs, as well as technical assistance in the formalization of the MFIs, to fill the gaps identified with a view to completing and following up on this as it is an essential part of ensuring the sustainability of funding of activities contributing to climate change mitigation.

## Output 3.1: Locally relevant financial instruments are developed to support low carbon climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry initiatives

The project will focus in particular, but not exclusively, on the stimulation of an affordable supply of credit suitable for the forestry and agricultural sectors. An increased supply of credit will support the development of agroforestry and forestry initiatives that are more resilient to climate change impacts. The project will target smallholder producers and associations, and small-sized initiatives carried out by smallholder producers and associations, and support the development of value chains.

The project will partner with national and local MFIs (MUCODECO, CAPPED, CODEC, COFIRA, Hope Congo and FCCECM) to develop microcredit and micro-finance products with conditions favourable to the adoption of climate-resilient agroforestry practices. The project will also support the development of a financial inclusion strategy for the forestry sector that will be elaborated or strengthened with project support (characterization of the MFIs are detailed in the Feasibility Study). This strategy will provide guidance on the design and implementation of microfinance institutions, which will include lines, and MFIs which will increase their portfolio.

### Activity 3.1.1: Identification and development of suitable credit lines for forestry and agricultural sectors

An in-depth analysis of a variety of potential micro-finance products and services suitable for local conditions will be carried out. Based on the findings of the analysis, targeted support will be provided to selected microfinance institutions for the development of new micro-credit lines. These lines will be designed with terms that are more favourable than those currently in place. In particular, a longer grace period (up to 12 months) and a lower interest rate will be proposed.

The project will also provide technical assistance over several years in periods of time, based on the needs identified by the MFIs. This technical assistance will facilitate the adoption of microfinance products and services that are more favourable for beneficiaries in the frame of the project to measure the impact of the project. The project will also support the development of a financial inclusion strategy for the forestry sector and will be able to carry out the activities. The project will monitor and evaluate the suitability of the new credit lines with respect to their accessibility by beneficiaries and initiate corrective actions as needed.

### Activity 3.1.2: Development of inclusive financial products and services for agri-food value chain (IFAD co-financing)

Under IFAD co-financing, the project will develop and mainstream inclusive financial products and services for agroforestry value chains promoted by PREFOREST under component 2. A mapping analysis will be conducted to identify relevant products and services that can be further adapted to the agri-food sector. The project will then work with

with relevant MFIs to adapt and mainstream the locally relevant agri-food financial products and services into selected MFI operations, which will include transportation, storage and transformation of the original crops and other agricultural commodities.

### Activity 3.1.3. Facilitation of Interactions between beneficiaries and national financial institutions for the effective implementation of business plans

The Project will support various interventions aimed at facilitating interactions between MFIs and producers adopting a market-oriented approach in order to provide them with access to credit facilities, as well as the implementation of robust business plans developed by the beneficiaries. Approximately 900,000 USD are targeted for this activity.

This intervention is expected to be implemented through the development of purchase agreements and sales platforms.

### Output 3.4. Access to market by the beneficiaries is facilitated through the development of purchase agreements and sales platforms

The Project will also aid producers to the market by supporting the development and implementation of long-term, fair price contracts with specific groups of beneficiaries and selected off-takers / agro-industrial enterprises in Congo through the establishment of a new platform for the sale of agricultural products.

Through this intervention, the formation of the PREFOREST (see F5 Annex 7) as well as supplementary work carried out in collaboration with the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, several potential buyers of specific crops from beneficiaries' farms have been identified. namely: i) company food enterprises; ii) institutional off-takers; iii) private off-takers; iv) public institutions; v) cooperatives.

The PREFOREST platform will be used to identify a list of specific co-markets/enterprises, the products in which they deal and their location are given in the feasibility study. Many enterprises have already indicated that some types of produce that they would purchase are already secured provided in the Annex 23 (Support letters from partners).

## Company food enterprises' market

This market consists of the local food systems represented by supermarkets and other retail outlets, which will be able to provide a stable demand for crops and commodities from beneficiaries farms sales to these actors. However, the fragmented nature of the local food value chain, presents many small-scale farmers with difficulties in accessing this market.

The following companies have expressed interest in purchasing crops directly from the project: i) Bazzaville; Hôpital États – Ponte; Noné; Grand Hôpital de Douala - Dolosse in each of the five departments in the Project area. ii) CACAO (Cacao) interested in purchasing cocoa beans and cocoa products from beneficiaries' farms. iii) COCAO interested in purchasing cocoa beans and cocoa products from beneficiaries' farms. iv) S.A. Interested in purchasing grassy crops (e.g. peanuts and soybeans) to produce vegetable oil. v) COPCAO interested in purchasing grassy crops (e.g. peanuts and soybeans) to produce vegetable oil.

Institutional arrangements will be established with the actors requiring transformation, such as cocoa and groundnuts. The project's groundnut production could be easily absorbed by Eco-Oil which is aiming to expand its operations in the country. In addition, the project has identified a potential market for 26,000 tonnes absorbed in 2015 (interest in project production has been confirmed in a letter of intent). COPACAO is ready to invest more than provided under the project in order to increase cocoa production and cocoa beans processing capacity. The main actors involved are: SCDIE, which will be the implementing agency for the project; based on this (this has also been indicated in the letter of intent); SCDIE is an important oil-farmer cooperative with 10,500 members. It is a major producer of cocoa beans and groundnuts, as well as the most important quantity of cassava, safou, plantain, avocado, orange and groundnuts.

PREFREST has also initiated detailed discussions with a dozen key stakeholders during the formulation phase in order to fine-tune the project's objectives: cocoa beans, cacao beans, safou, autogummé, furetwood and different fruits. During implementation, the project will link these midstream to producers through its market development strategy. The project will also work with the actors involved in the transformation process.

PREFREST has also initiated consultation with several local restaurants and chefs in each of the five departments in the project area during the formulation process. To strengthen networking and connection during implementation, the project will establish a network of 100 restaurants and 50 hotels that will be linked to food businesses to bring producers and restaurants closer together. The production of plantain, cassava, ora, autogummé, furetwood and other fruits will also be promoted.

The country needs on fuelwood is huge. For the supply of Brazzaville and Nkayi only, 47,500 ha additional plantations would be needed and the project objective could contribute to this need. To be noted that this need has been estimated during the formulation phase.

Based on the contacts already established with off-takers and others to be prospected during project implementation, it is expected that a significant number of many crops produced under the agro-forestry systems established by PREFREST, such as cassava, cocoa beans, etc., will be absorbed. PREFREST will thus facilitate the negotiation of prices and the signature and formalization of purchase agreements with these actors.

The project will draw on a "Forest and Farm Facility" (FFF) experience with respect to the establishment of market platforms.

- Access to information and market analysis: facilitating knowledge exchange and market intelligence among producers, who can work towards getting a better price for their products and identifying potential business opportunities. This is achieved through the use of a web-based platform that connects smallholder farmers across rural areas and crosschecked with market data from across the region and central level. This system is helping farmers to identify new markets and connect directly with buyers.

- Organization of local forest and farm producers (project beneficiaries) into strong groups, strengthening the horizontal linkages between such producers and their enterprises has tremendous potential for scaling up their efforts and creating thriving rural economies for the long term. The lack of such groups has been identified as a major barrier to market access and increased production. These groups are also more likely to demand existing animal feed.

- Negotiation of better terms on the supply chain based on fixed prices: More efficient results, especially increased profits, are observed when producers are grouped within associations or cooperatives. This is because they can negotiate with buyers in order to obtain the market information and obtain the technical support needed for successful partnership with potential off-takers.

### Activity 3.4.2: Support of local market platform and operationalization of purchase agreements with buyers (IPAD co-financing)

The Project will establish market platforms bringing together buyers and producers for the operationalization of purchase agreements. The project will also provide technical assistance to support the planning, design, and implementation of the market platform in terms of its structure, function, and supply, delivery and other relevant interventions to facilitate the effective implementation of agreements through the value chain. For example, PREFOREST, in association with MAFIP, will support the organization of commercialization platforms for cocoa, groundnut, and maize and will disseminate appropriate technology, etc., to communities.

General Selection Criteria for the Beneficiaries.

The beneficiaries (smallholder farmers) of the Project will be selected by the PMI according to the following criteria:

* Dependence in agriculture, natural resources and forest, and proximity** to forest area (potential actors for sustainable forest management);
* Low income level (up to 2 USD/day, as defined by the Ministry of Finance of Congo);
* Ownership or access rights over land with conservation potential;
* Vulnerability of key livelihoods to climate change impacts;
* Insecure land rights for women, indigenous peoples and smallholder farmers;
* No access to microfinance institutions;
* None or limited access to micro-finance institution; and
* Association membership.

## Selection criteria for MFIs

Identification of the potential MFI by PMU and in collaboration with stakeholders will be done through the following criteria:

* Credits allocated to cooperatives (not only individuals)

* Simplified process (with low or without financial guarantee).

## The Project Steering Committee (SC) will act as a decision-making organ and provide technical and strategic guidance by ensuring that links and appropriate coordination are maintained with relevant programmes/projects of all other United Nations agencies, as well as international environmental agreements, particularly the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), the Kyoto Protocol, the Paris Agreement, and the United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification (CCD). The SC will be chaired by the government (MEF), with FAO as co-chair. The SC will consist of representatives from relevant government agencies, development partners, strategic development partners, the GCF focal point and head of the Project Management Unit (Chief Technical Advisor), and other stakeholders involved in the implementation of the Project; ensure sound management and propose relevant recommendations. Decisions of the SC are binding on all parties.

### The Technical Committee (TC) will ensure the technical soundness of the Project by providing technical advice to the PMU and ensuring coordination of the implementation of the Project with government agencies. The decisions of the TC are not binding, but they should be followed as a reference for the PMU. The TC will meet at least once every 6 months.

The SC will consist of representatives from relevant government agencies, development partners, strategic development partners who will support implementation. If necessary, representatives of development partners, NGOs, research institutions and other stakeholders involved in the Project will attend meetings to consolidate the technical soundness of the Project. The TC will meet 4 times a year, once on each quarter.

The composition of these two committees is proposed as follows:

### Implementation Arrangements

FAO will serve both as the Accounting Entity (AE) and the Executing Entity (EE) for this Project, with a structure that encourages a high level of appropriation and implementation by the government and supports capacity building related to project activities. The Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development will be responsible for the overall coordination and the Project coordination functions. As the Executing Entity, FAO will take responsibility for the effective implementation of the Project activities in accordance with the approved work plan and budget, as well as the coordination of the planning and implementation of Project activities financed by GCF proceeds. The PREFOREST will be co-financed by the Government of Congo, FAO through CAFI resources and IFAD. CAFI and IFAD, according to their respective mandates, will provide technical assistance in the areas of project design, preparation, and comparative advantage in executing the core financing activities. The Ministry of Agriculture of the Republic of Congo will act as the host entity for the PREFOREST Project. The Ministry of Forest Economy will be responsible for Activity 3.2.1 (Activity 3.2.2, Activity 3.2.3, and Activity 3.4.2). FAO will execute Activity 2.5.1 co-financed by FAO through CAFI resources and IFAD. The Ministry of Forest Economy will be responsible for the implementation of the activities that apply, in alignment with GCF’s obligations under the AMA and with GCF regulations, rules and policies.


---
**Page 37**
---

developed at the beginning of each annual exercise through the development of complementary/combined annual workplans and approved budgets (AWPB). The FAO Country Office's relationships with the government, technical and financial partners (TFPs) and civil society organizations may be used in the implementation of this Project to ensure complementarity and avoid duplication. A Project Management Unit (PMU) will be established within the Project Management Unit (PMU) for the Project within the MEF premises to handle the coordination and implementation of the Project (day-to-day activities). The PMU will consist of three components: a Technical Coordination Advisor (CTA), who is a senior specialist, will provide expertise and material related to the Project's themes if necessary (agriculture, forestry, gender, research, etc.). The CTA will work with the National Focal Point (NFP) and other government entities as needed. The PMU will handle coordination and implementation of the entire Project. The role of the PMU is to ensure that work on the three components is coordinated as a whole so that activities will be responsive to the needs of the project, including those activities implemented by the various groups (Partners, NGOs, civil society organizations) and monitoring and evaluating compliance with safeguards. The PMU will work in close collaboration with various ministers and other government entities, as well as TFPs and civil society organizations. The CTA will receive technical assistance from FAO and will report to the Technical Committee (TC) and the Steering Committee (SC). The CTA will also be supported by a Project Management Unit (PMU), which consists of three components: an Advisor (CTA), an FAO staff member, who will have an overview of the Project. The CTA will work together with the National Focal Point (NFP) and other government entities as needed. The PMU will be responsible for monitoring and reporting to the CTA. The PMU will meet each week or more often if necessary, to monitor Project activities. PMU personnel will be provided by FAO and will be based in Addis Ababa, Ethiopia, where all resources are available to the Project.

Regular dialogue with the CAF governance bodies (Interministerial Committee, Steering Committee) and FAO PAE will be maintained through the PMU. This is to ensure that expected results and objectives, activities and workplans of all co-executing entities take into account contributions from parallel initiatives and are complementary rather than duplicative. The PMU will also maintain regular dialogue with other partners (e.g., TFPs) and civil society organizations in the Regional Office for Africa (RAF), as well as experts on innovation issues related to agroforestry, funding and value chain development. The PMU will also work closely with the CTA to ensure that the Project leverages knowledge and expertise from other regions in Africa, particularly west Africa where FAO is currently working with its members to develop understanding of the climate change mitigation options in forests and land use.

### During the project inception phase a grievance mechanism will be established at field level for the filing of complaints. Complaints will be recorded and reported to the PMU on a regular basis. The PMU will also report to the FAO on any grievances received and other related events throughout the project lifetime. This information will also be included with all awareness raising materials, including those that are disseminated in the communities. Reporting on any grievances received and how they were addressed as part of the monitoring of safeguards performance.

### Financial Flows:

FAO as the accredited entity (AE) will receive the funds from the GCF at HQ level. The funds will then be transferred to the FAO Representation in Congo, who will hire technical expertise for the PMU in charge of executing the Project. This will include the preparation and implementation of the monitoring and evaluation process.

FAO in its role of EE will manage project financial expenditures against budgets, execute payments, and provide technical assistance to the PMU in the execution of their mandates. The relevant GCF processes to be used for financing the project is accordance with the FAO and AMA. Accountability on the use of financial resources will be assured through the review of annual and bi-annual project reports, as well as through monitoring reports.
