Partners expected to receive GCF proceeds through funds transfer in accordance with FAO’s procurement roles are organizations involved in the Project implementation as procured parties (MEP, MAEP, etc., see procurement plan for more details). The technical and financial management capacity of procured partners will be assessed during the operational phase of the project. This assessment will be conducted by the GCF through a self-assessment tool (see Section 507). Some of the procured partners have already had a LoA with FAO, meaning that no additional capacity assessment is required for these partners. The funds transfer to procured parties will be made in accordance with FAO procedures and pursuant to the AMA signed with the GCF. More specifically, FAO will transfer funds to procured parties through the Project Management Unit (PMU) of the GCF (see Figure 9). This process (i.e., monitoring and evaluation) is to be followed by a competitive process in line with FAO rules and procedures.

![](image.png)

FAO will sign a Financial Agreement with the Government of Congo represented by the Ministry of Finance as recipient of the loan. The funds will be transferred from IFAD to FAO through an Inter-Agency Agreement (IAA) signed between FAO and IFAD. The funds will be disbursed in accordance with the terms and conditions set out in the IAA, including the following: (i) no pre-financing; (ii) no co-financing; (iii) no repayment of loans; (iv) no interest on loans; (v) no loan fees; (vi) no other expenses provided by the FAO (which is under the supervision of the Ministry of Agriculture as Executing Entity for the execution of the IFAD project); and (vii) no co-financing. The funds will be transferred to FAO through an IAA signed between FAO and IFAD Country Director. As such, the funds will not flow from the Ministry of Finance to the Ministry of Agriculture. The International Financial Institution (IFI) is responsible for disbursing the funds in accordance with the terms and conditions set out in the IAA. The disbursement of Congo contracts with IFID (independently of the kind of activities that are executed, which will not generate cash flow to repay a loan).

# B.S. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

The GCF’s financial support in the form of a grant is indispensable to effectively overcome obstacles to Congo's transition to a low-emission resilient development, specifically, the reduction of carbon emissions from slash-and-burn agriculture and deforestation. The country has limited fiscal space for climate action as public revenues are very limited. According to its NDC (2015), the funds needed to implement the low-carbon scenario are US$ 5.77 billion by 2030, of which US$ 4.89 billion is required in the agricultural sector.

The Investment Plan of the REDD+ National Strategy of the Republic of Congo, 2018-2025 estimates that approximately US$ 6.5 billion will be needed to implement this plan by 2030.

Congo is going through a very challenging period characterized by persistently weak macroeconomic fundamentals. The country has been facing a severe economic crisis since the beginning of 2018, which has led to a sharp decline in revenue. The prolonged drop in oil prices since 2014 has heavily affected its economy and led to a recession in 2016-2017. This situation was further exacerbated by the collapse of the price of cobalt in early 2018.

Budget deficits decreased significantly by almost 12% of GDP between 2016 and 2017 as a result of reductions in public spending, including on social services and infrastructure. The country has also been facing several shocks and different donors to external debt. Since the start of these negotiations, developed countries have generally provided support for the restructuring of Congo’s external debt.

Negotiations ended in July 2019 with a restructuring of Congo's external debt for an amount of USD 486 million. The agreement includes a moratorium on interest payments and a reduction of principal by 50%. This is the foundation for sustainable and inclusive growth. This situation has reduced the country’s ability to finance its climate change priorities, including those related to agriculture.

The GCF project will help Congo to overcome these obstacles and provide the necessary resources to support the foundations of sustainable and inclusive growth. This situation has reduced the country's ability to finance its climate change priorities, including those related to agriculture.

The PREFOREST project targets smallholder farmers and producers, earning less than 2 USD a day and will introduce activities that will generate enough profit to incentivize the farmers and be sustainable in the long term. The project will also provide technical assistance for the implementation of the land access and security law in the first instance, although it is anticipated that this situation will change once families have established the initial rights.

This project represents the first forestry GCF project in the Congo Basin and the first collaboration between the GCF and the Republic of Congo. It will be implemented by the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAF) and the MINECOF. The model proposed by the project has therefore the potential of being scaled up and replicated in other areas of the Congo Basin.

For Component 1, the GCF’s financial support will strengthen land access and security rights. In fact, the unplanned and informal nature of land tenure is a major obstacle to sustainable forest management and the effective implementation of natural resource management in general, and forest resources in particular. This translates into interdicted deactivation decisions by the local population.

For Component 2, the results of the various production systems under consideration by the Project clearly show that, without an external contribution in the form of a grant to cover the investment costs and certain operating costs, it would be impossible for smallholder farmers and producers to adopt climate-resilient agricultural practices. Conclusions produced with regard to climate-resilient agroforestry systems, the results of the financial analysis show that, even if the NPV is positive (10% in the case of cocoa and 25% in the case of coffee), it will not be until year 9/10th the first year) or cover to be viable. Although the NPV is positive, providing a grant cover part of the investment costs would allow producers to adopt these practices and thus contribute to reducing deforestation rates while also reducing the investment costs of orange and avocado-based systems. This is also because project beneficiaries are not able to access credit from financial institutions or other sources due to their low income levels. The project will remove these barriers to investment on the supply side, allowing producers to adopt production practices that will significantly reduce deforestation in the Congo Basin while contributing to the achievement of multiple benefits in terms of mitigation and adaptation. Overall, in the Republic of the Congo production costs are among the highest in Africa. For example, the official minimum wage in the Republic of the Congo is 90,000 FCFA (approx. 149 USD). In Côte d'Ivoire it is 65,000 FCFA (approx. 108 USD) and in Ghana it is 32,000 FCFA (approx. 54 USD).

The unit cost of the cocoa tree in the Republic of Congo is 79,000 FCFA (approx. 132 USD). In Sudan it is 3,000 Sudanese Pounds (approx. 54 USD).


---

which is fully costed, while family labour of these smallholder farmers may be provided for free or below commercial rates. The costs adopted by PREFOREST are very similar to those used for the REDD+ Investment fund of the country.

For Component 3, the informal and embryonic nature of the agricultural and fuelwood sector value chains is a major obstacle in terms of access to finance. This is due to the lack of formalization of these activities, which are mainly carried out by small producers. As a result, the agricultural sector represented only 6% of the Congolese cash sector portfolio in 2018. The project will therefore focus on supporting the development and diversification of the agricultural sector for solvency and profitability required by local financial institutions. In the current context, a grant from the GCF is almost the only source of financing available to support this sector. This will be used to finance the implementation of climate-resilient emission reduction activities. Financial support in the form of grants will enable an enabling environment conducive to the development and diversification of agricultural value chains through the strengthening of local financial institutions, as well as partnerships.

The project is aligned with the framework of the REDD+ National Strategy for Congo, which aims to create results-based payment systems for activities that reduce GHG emissions from deforestation and forest degradation. PREFOREST will generate results and support the strengthening of enabling conditions for the development of agricultural and fuelwood sector value chains in the context of climate change.

### B. Exit strategy and sustainability (max. 500 words, approximately 7 pages)

The project is designed to be self-sustaining through the responsibility of the supporting institutions' appropriation of funding by the local financial sector, and Congo's approach to payment for ecosystem services in general and REDD+ results-based payments in particular. The PREFOREST will provide the initial investment needed in order to support the development of agricultural value chains and diversification of fuelwood activities that are currently invest in low carbon activities. After the initial phase, the Project will follow a performance-based financial sustainability strategy. This will be achieved by generating revenues from the sale of products produced on the farm in plan in order to receive further financial support from the Project. The financial viability of activities will allow becoming self-sustaining and thus reduce the need for additional funding from the project. The project will also rely on a linkage between the beneficiaries and Congolese financial institutions, as well as an increased supply of credit. To this end, the project will provide training to farmers in order to improve their skills and knowledge related to agricultural and forestry production cycles, which will allow beneficiaries to obtain the necessary credit to finance their activities.

The project will also support the development of a market for products produced on farms at the start of their activities, which will be referred to them (if they benefit so desires) as the end of the Project, with all proceeds from sales being reinvested in the project. The project will also provide training and technical assistance to farmers.

PREFOREST has already received letters of intent from WFP, ECO CIFOR, CEFACOD, EPFAURA, SCDE, Agrodes, AGRIFIN, FONAFIFO, GIZ, IUCN, WWF, CONGO-ECO, and the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry. These institutions are financial institutions (Hipe, Modaco, Codex, CEFACOD and CIFOR) who confirm their willingness to engage and partner with the project to ensure the sustainability of the interventions.

The project is aligned with the framework of the REDD+ National Strategy for Congo, which aims to create results-based payment systems for activities that reduce GHG emissions from deforestation and forest degradation. PREFOREST will generate results and support the strengthening of enabling conditions for the development of agricultural and fuelwood sector value chains in the context of climate change.

conditions will not support the request to oppose REDD+ RBPs. Cong will explore the future SGP REEDS (REDD+ RBP) program amongst other options. Congo's monitoring period started in January 2013 and all emission reductions (except those from the implementation of entire PREFOREST funded activities which will be retired) could apply if the country continues to meet its REDD+ RBPs.

Another fundamental element related to institutional sustainability is institutional capacity building and administrative support for the implementation of the project. The PREFOREST project will contribute to the strengthening of several key ministries, including MEF, MAEP, MTEF, MAIFDP and MRSET for the implementation of certain Project interventions. The Project will strengthen the capacities of PROHAN and SNR (at central level and at the level of the departments) as well as the MAFD, MAIFDP and MRSET. It also includes the strengthening of the capacity of the development committees (CMDCs), as well as departmental directorates of land affairs. It also consists of building the capacities of the private sector actors involved in the project through training sessions and workshops. The slash-and-burn agriculture, specifically through the development of profitable business plans, strengthening of partnerships between small-scale producers and agro-industrial companies, and the creation of a revolving fund for financing. All stakeholders will then be better equipped to sustain the Project's benefits and to effectively conduct other parallel projects in the future.

Finally, PREFOREST together with its partners (CAFI and FAAD mainly) will be catalytic for the development of a programmatic approach for long-term investments on climate change mitigation and adaptation actions at national level. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the National Strategy on Climate Change by agroforestry-based activities with interventions that include among others, strengthening of market access (through the creation of a revolving fund for financing), facilitation of access to credit facilities (including microfinance markets) and facilitation of long-term, inclusive off-taker partnerships between small-scale producers (and their cooperatives) and agro-industrial companies. The project will also contribute to the mobilization of public and financial partners, as well as from other new actors mainly from the private sector (including micro-finance institutions). In this way, PREFOREST will be a key actor in the implementation of the National Strategy on Climate Change at national level.

The project does not propose implementation of a results-based payment system during its lifespan. However, the government decided to implement a pilot phase of such a system for the period 2013-2015 and to continue it beyond this period with the relevant stakeholders and in accordance with its REDD+ National Strategy and the resulting benefit sharing procedure.

## C.3 Capacity building and technology development/transfer (max. 250 words; approximately 0.5 page)

* C.3.1 Does GCF funding finance capacity building? Yes ☑️ No ☐

* C.3.2 Does GCF funding finance technology development/transfer? Yes ☑️ No ☐

Capacity building at individual and institutional levels and technology development/transfer are strategic interventions in Component 2.

For Component 2, the project will provide significant institutional support in terms of building the capacities of government structures such as MEP (e.g. PRODRAR, SNIS, MAEP, MAPFOP, and MTE). This institutional support will be achieved through training for staff on the use of information technology tools and training in its use. Beneficiaries will be trained in all aspects of the climate-resilient agriculture system, including the use of improved seeds, agroforestry systems, and the establishment of nurseries, mainly through farmer field schools with technical assistance from SNR officials and MEP sector chiefs.

With regard to the development and transfer of technologies, innovative and locally relevant climate-resilient agroforestry and agricultural systems will be developed to stabilize slash-and-burn agriculture and to provide a sustainable livelihood for smallholder farmers. The project will also support the development of improved yields and sustainability of the promoted production systems. The IRF and IRA will play a central role here, particularly in terms of providing technical assistance and training on the use of the technologies. This will be supported by an indicative estimation of implementation costs and associated revenue. Support for technology transfer will target beneficiaries working to adopt innovative systems like the Acacia auriculiformis agroforestry system developed in Manra, DRC.

## D. PROJECT IMPLEMENTATION (approximately 5 page)

### A. Project Description (approximately 1 page)

The main objective of the Project is the mitigation in the form of reduced carbon emissions from land use, land-use change and forestry activities through the establishment of agroforestry and forest systems on a total area of approximately 10,000 ha.

#### B. Expected Results

- **GHG Reductions**: The project will result in a direct reduction in emissions of approximately 8.4 million tonnes of CO2 equivalent (tCO2e) per year when the Project is fully implemented.
- **Carbon Sequestration**: GHG reductions were estimated in accordance with the guidelines of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) and using appropriate methodologies and tools to take into account the carbon sequestration/absorption potential of the different Project interventions.

#### C. Project Implementation

For the planned climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry models, estimations were made using the Ex-Ante Carbon Benefits Tool (EACBT), which is a tool that estimates the carbon benefits from the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices such as agroforestry practices, as well as the use of improved varieties of species that are more tolerant of the anticipated changes in climatic conditions. The EACBT also takes into account the potential for increased yields and income through the dissemination and adoption of several climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry models, allowing the local people to diversify their livelihoods and increase their resilience to climate change.

The Project will also contribute significantly to the reduction of vulnerability and strengthening of the climate change adaptation capacities of at least 417,733 direct beneficiaries (14,408 women and indigenous populations), as well as 678,649 indirect beneficiaries (25,000 farmers and smallholder farmer groups composed of women, young people and indigenous populations). The Project will also contribute to the reduction in vulnerability and strengthening of climate change adaptation capacities of at least 13,331 direct beneficiaries (4,500 farmers and smallholder farmer groups composed of women, young people and indigenous populations), as well as 28,679 indirect beneficiaries (10,000 farmers and smallholder farmer groups composed of women, young people and indigenous populations).

The Project will have a transformative change in the forestry and forest-related sectors by establishing the necessary enabling conditions to effectively shift the course of high-carbon footprint agricultural and fuelwood production practices into low-carbon footprint ones.

With regards to the development and adoption of innovative technologies for agriculture production and fuelwood production, the Project will facilitate the dissemination and adoption of climate-resilient agroforestry systems (such as 14,500 ha), such as the Acacia auriculiformis agroforestry system and Tamarindus indica agroforestry system. The economic co-benefits generated by the Project will specifically revolve around the creation of green jobs and alternative livelihoods for women in all project activities. The high cost of the price of oil, which represents 90% of government revenue, have deprived the government of important financial resources needed to create or maintain employment opportunities. In addition, the economic crisis, characterized by a decrease in public spending, budget reduction and reallocation to more pressing issues, has also included important hardship on the private sector and small-scale farmers. The Project will contribute to the creation of green jobs for women in all project activities and agriculture. GCF investment under the PREFREST offers a unique opportunity to reverse this situation through the creation of alternative livelihoods for women in all project activities. This will be achieved by promoting climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry from the implementation of the PREFREST. An important number of these green jobs are expected to be created in the districts targeted under SDG 2 (the Project area). The Project will also contribute to ensure the sustainable growth of the abovementioned sectors. Furthermore, the deployment of climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry systems and structures of value chains with women as key actors will contribute to the diversification of cassava-maize or cocoa-palm-nuts-peanuts will benefit from alternatives source of more or less resilient livelihoods.

**Page 50**

The results of the Project’s feasibility study revealed important needs for institutional capacity building and improved coordination among various stakeholders to ensure effective implementation of the proposed activities. For example, the MAEP is supposed to facilitate access by smallholder farmers and smallholders’ groups to credit facilities provided under the National Forest Sector Investment Plan (NFSIP) – FSA created in 2005. Unfortunately, the FSA only received an average investment budget of approximately USD 2 million per year over its lifetime. The same applies for the PAFAS created in 1986 and the PAFFS created in 1989, which are responsible for monitoring, coordination and implementation of the national reforestation programme. These programmes have been hampered by a lack of resources, outdated institutional structures, dilapidated infrastructure and a total mismatch between the current staff numbers and the mandates assigned to this unit. The PAFAS is also supposed to provide technical assistance in the implementation of the NFSIP in compliance with the objectives owing to budgetary constraints. Like most research institutions, annual budget allocations are insufficient for the smooth functioning of the institution. This has led to a deterioration of operations of the institution, to the diminution of assigned research activities. For example, several research programmes have been suspended or delayed due to lack of funding. These include the Programme d’Action pour l’Agroforesterie et les Plantations Forestières (PAFFS) – as well as some ongoing programmes that need to be consolidated. It also means that only 20% of the permanent staff and research and research technicians represent more than half of the total personnel permanent, which is very low for a research institution.

### D. Country ownership (max. 350 words; approximately |1 page|)

The Congo has been actively involved in the implementation of the REDD+ NDC in Congo, particularly the GHG emission mitigation strategy structured around two major axes, notably: (i) The fight against unplanned deforestation (REDD+) and (ii) The promotion of sustainable forest management practices, including reforestation and afforestation. With respect to adaptation, the measures include: (i) the protection of production systems sensitive to climate change; (ii) the development of strategies for the conservation of biodiversity in protected areas, preventing forest degradation, and preventing fires; and (iii) the transfer of climate technologies adapted to national conditions.

The Project will also contribute to the effective implementation of the REDD+ National Strategy (REDD+ NS) identified as a priority under the Congo’s National Development Plan (NPD). The NPD identifies the following strategic option (number 1): improvement of agricultural systems (strategic option #3), rationalization of fuelwood consumption and promotion of alternative energy sources (strategic option #4), and the development of forest-based products. The NPD also identifies the GCF as a potential financial partner for implementation of the REDD+ over the period 2018-2025 in the form of co-financing with other partners, including the World Bank, the European Union, bilateral donors, technical and financial partners, who are heavily involved in issues related to climate change, REDD+ and adaptation. The Project is in line with the Congo’s priorities in terms of low-emission climate resilient development as indicated by the National Development Plan (NPD) 2018-2022, which revolves around six fundamental pillars including: improving agricultural systems; rationalizing fuelwood consumption and promoting alternative energy sources; supporting the agricultural sector; pillar RZ specifically makes provision for the dissemination of high-performance production technologies in agriculture; pillar S specifically makes provision for the development of a national strategy for the sustainable management of forest resources in the fight against climate change in the forestry sector, including the promotion of agroforestry and the rationalization of fuelwood consumption. The Investment Plan of the REDD+ National Strategy, specifically focusing on improving agricultural systems and rationalizing fuelwood consumption.

The GCF Focal Point for Congo was heavily involved in the Project formulation process, from the concrete note of interest to the final project document. The GCF Focal Point provided technical support during the preparation of the strategic guidance on the Project scope and interventions along the way. Observations and comments from the expert panel were incorporated in the final version of the funding proposal. Furthermore, the expert panel will be closely involved throughout the implementation phase.

The number of senior permanent staff is very low (only 20%) and there are a lack of senior technical personnel to carry out the research activities. This is a serious problem for young people to ensure continuity of the structure.

### D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The financial analysis carried out in the EFA took into account all the benefits from the Project interventions for society as a whole, including the economic, social and environmental benefits and costs of the Project's interventions from the perspective of society as a whole. Details of the assumptions on which this analysis is based are provided below.

The quantitative benefits taken into account in the EFA are the incremental net benefits of the establishment of climate-resilient agroforestry systems over 14,500 ha taking account of the enhanced value chains supported by marketing and distribution systems proposed by the Project.

Two discount rates were used for the EPA: 20% and 25%. Discount rates of 10% and 6% were used for the financial and economic analysis respectively (a detailed explanation on the way these rates were selected is included in the EFA).

For climate-resilient agroforestry systems, the results of the financial analyses are included in the table below. The two models that are expected to be most popular are the so called Mampu systems, which are based on a fast-growing tree species (Mampru) and a slow-growing tree species (Pouya). The analysis shows that both the Mampu models (based on acacia) require a grant in order to be viable. Without a grant, the NPV is negative for all the models except for the Mampu model based on pouya. However, even with a 5% price of chaff, the NPV becomes positive and the project becomes attractive without any grants. More precisely, for the Acacia/pouya/grassland model at 5% price of chaff makes the NPV positive. For the Acacia/mampru/grassland model, even with a 10% price of chaff, the NPV is still negative and the project remains unattractive.

Although the NPV is positive for other models, a grant to cover part of the investment cost is necessary as credit is not available from banks or MFIs. The analysis shows that only a fraction of loans provided by banks and MFIs are for agricultural banks in the country. There are no agricultural banks in the country. In addition, there is a significant underdevelopment of the banking sector with respect to the promotion of agriculture or forestry activities in the country.

These considerations are at the basis of the chosen support, which consists in non-refundable grants to cover part of the investment cost and credit facilities for farmers. The grant will be provided by the GCF. Credit facilities will be provided by banks and MFIs.

The sensitivity analysis included in the EFA (See Annex 2) shows that these two Mangup systems models are not without risk (while all other models are much less sensitive to changes in costs and revenues). A fairly substantial reduction of revenue from the sale of maize would result in a negative NPV for both models. This is because the project would also happen in case of an cost increase of 18% for the acacia/groundnut/cassava system or of 15% for the acacia/cocoa/plantain system. The sensitivity analysis included in the EFA (See Annex 2) shows that these two models can assess the worthiness of models in a robust way. These assumptions include the exclusive use of paid labor to conduct all activities, and the exclusion of any potential benefits from the use of family labor (including seasonal labor especially for smallholders), which will increase the NPV of proposed investments (making the investment less sensitive to changes in costs). The sensitivity analysis also shows that the project would not be able to cover the project will fund agro-forestry investments requested by applicants on the basis of business plans (developed by smallholder farmers and/or other stakeholders, including cooperatives), which have to include a sensitive analysis. The business plans showing high risks will not be funded.

These analyses show that the results of the planned interventions (with grant) are sufficiently high to justify the Project justifying the Project’s intervention in these areas. However, it should be noted that the economic analysis does not take into account the GHG emission reduction is taken into account. Even without considering this, with a 25-year time horizon the economic net benefit of the project would be $108 million. The economic analysis also assumes that the Project will achieve the assumption that 73% of the 14 kg ha of targeted agroforestry systems include Mangup systems (based on acacia or cocoa) and 27% of the 14 kg ha of targeted agroforestry systems are planted with avocados and vegetables (6%) and oranges mixed with annual staple food crops (8%). The economic analysis also assumes that the Project will reduce GHG emissions by 0.84 million t CO2e per year. Considering the price level for carbon credits of USD 5 per tonne of CO2e, in this scenario, the economic analysis assumes a price of USD 5 per t CO2e. With this price level, the results of the economic analysis are included in the table below.

## E

### Results and Outputs

a) Quality control of Project monitoring and reporting to the GCF: The FAO will assume these responsibilities in line with the detailed provisions listed in the Accommodation Memorandum.

b) Monitoring and evaluation of the Project activities: The FAO will be responsible for the overall monitoring and evaluation of the Project, including the preparation of annual and six-monthly Project reports as well as monitoring reports. A Project Performance Monitoring System (PMU) will be developed based on the target indicators and objectives defined in the logical framework.

### Responsibilities

The PMU will be established by the FAO in collaboration with the Project implementing entities and will be managed by the PMU in collaboration with the implementing entities and in line with the performance monitoring plan. The PMU will be responsible for generating general coordination of the monitoring and evaluation activities of the various Project implementing entities, and will report to the GCF as required.

### Monitoring and Evaluation

The FAO will establish a monitoring and evaluation system in order to evaluate preparedness for effective implementation of the Project and associated procedures, particularly the establishment of the PMU, opening of an account with the GCF, signing of the project agreement, signing of purchase agreements, etc. This review will serve as a basis for the establishment of its PMUs.

A monitoring and evaluation expert will be recruited as part of the PMU to strengthen monitoring activities; a consultant will be hired by the FAO to provide technical assistance in the preparation of monitoring reports (the National Coordinator and Lead Technical Officer (LTO)). These monthly reports will be sent by the LTO to the FAO Office of Evaluation (EOE) for review. The EOE will evaluate the progress made, identify any implementation, implementation procedures and results obtained; (ii) Verify the conformity of the activities with the GCF and the logical framework; (iii) Identify lessons learned from the experience of the type of project and related issues; (iv) Monitor implementation of decisions from previous monitoring missions; (v) Resolve any other problem related to implementation of the project.

### Evaluation

To provide an external viewpoint on the progress of the Project and the achievement of its objectives, the FAO Office of Evaluation (EOE) will conduct two project evaluations: a preliminary review (interim) and a final review.

#### Interim Review

The interim evaluation will be conducted approximately 6 months after the start of activities in order to produce the first Project report for submission to the GCF. This final review will be conducted at the end of the Project, i.e., within 12-18 months from the start of project implementation.

#### Final Evaluation

A final Project evaluation will be conducted by the FAO Office of Evaluation approximately 6 months after the end of activities in order to produce the final Project report for submission to the GCF. This final review will be conducted at the end of the Project, i.e., within 12-18 months from the start of project implementation.

The interim evaluation will provide a complete evaluation of performance in terms of the targets and indicators of the logical framework, as well as the strengthening of the implementation provisions or amend the Project design. The final evaluation will be used to strengthen the implementation provisions or amend the Project design. During this review, there will be specific emphasis on the lessons learned by the FAO Office of Evaluation on the results and disadvantages.

### Final Evaluation

A final Project evaluation will be conducted approximately 6 months after the end of activities in order to produce the final Project report for submission to the GCF. This final review will be conducted at the end of the Project, i.e., within 12-18 months from the start of project implementation.

#### Evaluation Components

The evaluation will cover two main components: (i) Evaluate the benefits and impacts of the project for all products; and (ii) Identify incomplete activities and problems that have not been resolved by the end of the Project.

### In Line with the AEA

In line with the AEA, the FAO Office of Evaluation (EOE) will be in charge of the interim and final evaluation of the project. The evaluations will be conducted using an issue-based approach and may include evaluations using general criteria for assessing the effectiveness of the project. The results from these evaluations will be used to improve performance, as well as the sustainability and scaling up of the results obtained and the lessons learned. The evaluations will also provide recommendations on how to improve future projects in terms of design, implementation tools. In addition to the primary data collected by evaluators and the secondary national data available, the interim and final evaluation will be based on information provided by the Steering Committee, the Project Management Team, and, more specifically, the FAO. The interim progress reports in a format approved by the GCF’s Performance Monitoring Unit (PMU) will include: a) The main project progress product measured using performance indicators; b) The main implementation problems and solutions; c) An updated purchase

## Monitoring of forest carbon stocks. Monitoring of reductions in land-based emissions will be carried out by the CGIAR-CCAFS project, in collaboration with the FAO and FAO longest monitoring experts. Use will be made of the country's National Forest Monitoring System: Monitoring and Management, Reporting and Verification (NFMIS - M & R V). The Project area will be divided into 13 districts, each district will have a baseline emission factor that is monitored based on historical emissions analyzed for the 13 districts during the Project preparatory phase, established one year before the start of the project. Monitoring of forest carbon stocks in the Project area will be carried out by producing maps and evaluating activity data. The Project monitoring and evaluation system, particularly in terms of emission factors, will be based on the results of the baseline emissions analysis conducted for the 13 districts during the development of the baseline. The CGIAR and PMU will establish a forest reference level (FRL) for the Project area using the methodology described above. This FRL, on top of the use of the EX-ACT tool, will be used to monitor reductions in GHG emissions in the Project area.

### Monitoring of gender. The procured partners will support the implementation and monitoring of the Gender Action Plan (GAP). The PMU will be responsible for supervising and monitoring all activities related to gender, particularly updates of the GAP. The PMU will also be responsible for ensuring that the results of the monitoring are communicated locally to the Ministry for the Promotion of Women and the Integration of Women in Development for the approval of project activities related to gender, monitoring and sustainment.

### Data disaggregation. Data will be collected on age-relevant and gender-specific indicators which will be integrated in the PMMS. Data disaggregated by gender will specifically focus on activities related to the specific objectives of the Project, such as the empowerment of women and girls, and the inclusion of youth.

#### Monitoring of environmental and social safeguards. On the basis of the GCF checklist for rapid evaluation of environmental and social impact, the Project is globally classified in the category "Moderate" using the RAO criteria. The PMU will be responsible for supervising implementation of the Environmental and Social Framework Plan (ESFP) and the ESFP describes the necessary measures to ensure that the project complies with the requirements of the GCF and as well as the national laws and regulations. It consists of mitigation, monitoring and reporting on environmental and social safeguards.

#### Ultimately, this framework will be used as an anchor for beneficiaries, who will need to develop an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP).

The PMU will be responsible for monitoring implementation of the ESFP and ESMPs, but ultimately responsibility will be shared with the beneficiaries, who will need to ensure compliance with the ES environmental and social safeguards during project implementation. The Technical Consultant (TC) and the Project Specialist (SS) contracted by the PMU, will analyze and classify beneficiary initiatives using FAO categories (low, intermediate or high risk). The TC and SS will also be responsible for monitoring the implementation of the environmental and social management plan. For initiatives in category "low" or "C", he/she will produce a summary of results to be reported to the PMU. For initiatives in category "intermediate" or "high", he/she will ensure that: (i) The ESMIP results are communicated locally; (ii) The ESMIP reports are submitted to the Technical Committee for review and approval, and (iii) The ESMIP results are incorporated into contracts with beneficiaries and that the service providers provide the required environmental management plans and/or comply with the grievance mechanisms.

In order to carry out this work effectively, the TC/SS will establish sub-committees with a support mandate in order to validate methodologies and provide advice on different environmental and social issues to be taken into account. The TC/SS will also be responsible for ensuring that all beneficiaries have undergone verifications in order to validate compliance with the environmental and social management plans developed by the beneficiaries.

FAO's Financial Regulators provide that the External Auditor shall be the Audit-Generator (or person exercising an equivalent function) of a Member Nation, selected through a transparent bidding process by FAO's Funding Bodies. The Funding Proposer will be managed by FAO as an AE and co-EE in compliance with FAO financial rules and regulations (shared with GCF during the accreditation process).

E. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

**Risk Factor 1:** Land access and security risk compromising the large-scale adoption of climate-smart agroforestry and forestry activities (page 73)

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | Medium |

Description:

According to the traditional systems of agricultural land access control in Congo, only families who are "land owners" have the right to plant trees on their land. Non-land owners, who generally practice subsistence agriculture and slash-and-burn farming, do not have this right. However, they often have the right to plant trees or claim lineage to land as the most common method of land acquisition.

The term "lineage" refers to a given piece of land that is therefore recognized for members of the family line who hold these rights. Many of us surveyed, working informally, often impelled fulfillment of land owners' wishes to secure their land. Social conflicts and disputes over land ownership are common in Congo. In some cases, non-land owners and agricultural producers with no land access right to the land they were cultivating revealed possible solutions for this problem by signing long-term contracts, which could allow for the integration of trees, is still not current practice in Congo, but certain examples exist.

In the case of customary land tenure systems, where land rights are based on tradition, they are not frozen—they develop with time. Traditionally, land is not sold; there are exceptions, particularly when a family moves to another village or when a person dies and leaves no heirs. Sometimes, the person who has cleaned the forest for the first time retains certain rights on hay fields.

(Mitigation Measures)

An important element in effective implementation of climate-smart agroforestry is the lease of land security. After in-depth analyses, the Project retained three options to remedy the issue of land access and security notably: (A) ensuring that PRONAT and state farmers have a legal framework for leasing their land; (B) allowing former State farmers. To date, almost 6,000 ha have been secured by PRONAT and lots of land are currently being leased to private individuals or companies; (C) the system of long-term contracts that allows non-land owners to make his land available to climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry activities by beneficially through long-term contracts to be negotiated jointly or exchange for shared remuneration. Unfortunately, PRONAT and state land are not leased out.

Strengthening of land access and security rights is a cornerstone of Components 1 and 2. Land access and security rights are critical in the implementation of climate-smart agroforestry and forestry activities. Strengthening of land access and security rights will specifically target non-land owners. In case for land owners to sign long-term contracts, which provides access to non-land owners or even signed by themselves.

**Note:** A type of real estate contract specifying that the lease must improve the property with construction.

## Selected Risk Factor 2 Marginalized groups do not benefit equally from Project activities

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |

### Description

Traditional land systems grant more powers and rights to men than women, despite the matrilineal nature of the social organization. Women are often excluded from decision-making processes in villages. In some cases, three or even several generations of indigenous populations. Nearly all land owners are Barwu men. Women typically inherit their husband’s land after his death. If a woman marries another man and her husband dies, she loses her land access rights to the new husband. The wife of a husband, the woman loses her land access rights. Indigenous populations have even less access to land and they are never land owners in the Project area. Within this context, without implementing concrete policies that ensure equal access to land for women and indigenous people, there is a risk that the project will not be implemented as intended. The Project will need to work with local communities to identify appropriate mechanisms to address these issues.

### After broad consultation, the Project developed a Framework Plan for Environmental and Social Management, a Gender Action Plan and a Planning Framework for Indigenous Peoples, which precisely define the targeted beneficiaries of the climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry initiatives. The project will ensure that women are not marginalized. In this sense, equitable sharing of the Project benefits involves not only activities requiring access to land but also those related to business development and value chains (Component 3). For example, in the specific case of Component 3, the Project will ensure that women have access to three land access opportunities identified by the Project for men, women, young people and indigenous populations.

The Project will favor the most vulnerable beneficiaries in local communities: smallholder farmers and will ensure that they benefit from the project (Component 1). Moreover, the most vulnerable beneficiaries, particularly women and indigenous peoples, will benefit from targeted capacity building activities. The Project will also ensure that the training provided is appropriate to the local context in order to guarantee their effective application. Two targets were set: a) 30% of project investments will be allocated for the benefit of women and indigenous populations; b) the number of beneficiaries should be proportional to the percentage of indigenous peoples in the affected villages.

With regard to the climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry initiatives, the Project plans to support interested beneficiaries in

## Selected Risk Factor 3
The initial investment risk becoming higher as increasingly attractive crops could increase the pressures and risks of deforestation and forest degradation.

### Category: Other
#### Probability: Low
#### Impact: Select

**Description:** One of the consequences of increasing the profitability of agricultural and climate-resilient agroforestry systems throughout the world is an increased interest in conversion from savanna land into agricultural land[1]. Congo has not remained on the fringes of this reality, and the deployment of the climate-resilient agroforestry systems considered here could lead to deforestation and forest degradation. This risk is particularly relevant in the case of production systems based on cocoa crops, which are currently the most profitable for farmers in Congo. The data indicate that it is by far the most promising production system. Deforestation in favor of cocoa crops was more than double between 2015-2016 and 2017-2018 compared to other agricultural systems. It should be noted that such a phenomenon may be replicated in Congo if appropriate mitigation measures are not implemented.

### Mitigation Measure(s):
Agriforestry systems within the context of forest areas will only be deployed within the context of local development plans. As such, these interventions in forest areas will be implemented in geographical spaces solely dedicated to this purpose and will not lead to deforestation or forest degradation. The size of the cultivated sites is, in addition, the size of the funded initiatives will be limited to 5 ha in forest areas; and each initiative must include a buffer zone that does not exceed 10% of the total area. Specifically, these restrictions are not causal and do not call for deforestation or forest degradation. The restrictions do not apply to areas where cocoa production systems pose no direct danger to the forest. Furthermore, the Project will conduct awareness-raising activities with local communities in order to ensure that practices are consistent with agricultural production and climate-resilient agroforestry.

### Selected Risk Factor 4
The project will support production systems varying from a minimum of 10 ha in savanna areas and 5 ha in forested areas. The Project will also support initiatives that contribute at least 10% to the investment cost. All these preconditions will limit the participation of a large part of the population, including marginal groups, whose current cropping practices are below this threshold (approximately less than 5 ha [generally 1.2 hectares]), with group farming being registered and without subsidies to cover the cost of their contribution, which is deemed prohibitive.

### Mitigation Measure(s):
To achieve the targets set for the participation of women and indigenous peoples, the Project will, if necessary, amend the minimum size requirements or the contribution payment conditions for groups of women or indigenous people. The amendment will be made in such a way that it does not exceed 10 ha in savanna areas; approximately 2 ha or even exceeding 5 ha in mechanized areas. The Project may also open up to informal groups, which are currently excluded from the project. These amendments will allow the Project to bring together people motivated by learning about climate-resilient agroforestry, not to be serviced by state assistance schemes, and who have no other means of income.

[1] Tiffen et al., 2015, Making Roads for Sustainable Intensification without Externalization - USAID
[2] Website of the International Tropical Agriculture

## Selected Risk Factor 5: Development of the management capacities of members of beneficiary groups will be critical, especially for women and indigenous peoples.

### Category: Technical and operational
- **Probability:** Medium
- **Impact:** Low

**Description:** The level of quality required by the Project for selected beneficiaries of agroforestry and forestry initiatives could prove to be a barrier to their participation if application development techniques are poorly mastered by the beneficiaries. This is particularly true in the case of women, who often lack the necessary skills and resources to meet these requirements. Women and indigenous peoples are the least capable of meeting these requirements. Management capacity development will affect other areas of the project, including planning activities and developments of national financial institutions.

### Mitigation Measures:
- The Project will aim to accurately match capacities of beneficiaries with training and other technical assistance through capacity needs assessments tailored to different stakeholder groups.
- The Project will assign members of each beneficiary group to a mentor who will provide guidance on the development and implementation of their respective business plans over the course of their activities. To this end, the Project will organize workshops for effective farm management; specific training and support will be organized for women and indigenous peoples.

## Selected Risk Factor 6: Indigenous peoples risk not benefiting sufficiently from the Project

### Category: Technical and operational
- **Probability:** Medium
- **Impact:** Low

**Description:** Indigenous peoples (IP) are victims of much discrimination by the Bantu populations. IP are never landowners, they have no access to credit or other financial services, and they are not represented in any decision-making structures; they are never successful with the village chiefs and they are not integrated into decision-making structures.
- Indigenous groups in existing projects rarely benefit from equitable sharing of benefits. It would be particularly difficult for IP to meet the minimum eligibility conditions for funding of initiatives and their greatest difficulty would be to ensure that they have access to the resources necessary to implement them, which is something that they could benefit from the support offered by the Project.

### A Planning Framework for Indigenous Peoples in line with the GCF and FAO safeguards was developed within the context of feasibility studies for this Project. This plan specifically identifies the obstacles to effective participation of indigenous peoples in the Project activities and proposes measures aimed at ensuring their active participation in the Project activities.
- The Project will pay particular attention to ensuring that the percentage of indigenous peoples benefiting from the Project is proportional to the percentage of the total population they represent within each beneficiary group. It is important for the percentage of indigenous peoples benefiting from the Project to be proportional to the percentage of the total population they represent in each beneficiary group.
- The Project will implement specific measures for IP, particularly through training and securing in their land access and other resources. These measures are aimed at ensuring that IP have a greater share of benefits than other beneficiaries, who will have to cover part of the initial investment costs for their initiatives. The Project will consider the need to provide additional financial support to IP, as well as to women and youth. The Gender and Social Specialist will be hired to take into account and address specific issues faced by indigenous peoples.

## Selected Risk Factor Y: The level of governance of the technical and administrative government departments hinders effective implementation of the Project.

### Reliability | Impact
- **Technical and operational** | Medium | Low

#### Description
Good governance is a major concern in Congo, which has a classified 100% on 180 countries on the Transparency International Corruption Perception Index. This governance issue is particularly evident in the implementation of development projects such as this one. In fact, during feasibility surveys, the consulted stakeholders expressed their concerns about the lack of transparency and accountability in the project. They also had doubts about impartiality in analyzing and selecting applicants for the climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry initiatives that will be supported. The low level of governance could hinder the effective implementation of the Project.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The Project will strengthen: transparency, by encouraging applications by initiators, through dissemination of the selection criteria, publication of the selection results and reasons for non-selection; as well as by disseminating information on the selection committee's composition. The Project will also support the analysis and selection of applicants. Education of local populations, dissemination of the selection criteria, and publication of the selection results and reasons for non-selection will be conducted through community meetings and forestry initiatives will be offered by pro-poor parties, particularly NGOs. A local committee will conduct an impact assessment on the project's implementation in order to ensure that it is implemented according to the selection criteria.

The primary analysis and selection of applications to be funded will be conducted by a mixed committee in the PMR. There will be members from ministries, the FAO and independent experts. The Safeguard Specialist required for this project will provide technical assistance on safeguard issues to the Project's steering subcommittees or recourse to other expertise. The selection committee will prepare a written justification on the basis of which it will select applicants. A detailed description of the selection criteria and rationale will be sent to the applicants and support will be given to applicants in reviewing rejected applications.

## Selected Risk Factor Z: Pesticide and chemical agricultural inputs could have adverse effects on the health of populations and the environment

### Category | Probability | Impact
- **Governance** | Low | Low

#### Description
Pesticide and chemical fertilizer use in Congo is relatively low owing to its unreliability and high cost, but it is necessary for smallholder farmers. The Project will not promote the use of pesticides or chemical fertilizers, but do not eliminate the need for their use. Agricultural producers have very little information on the effective use of agricultural chemicals integrated. Pest and pest management is underdeveloped in Congo.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The Project has developed an Environmental Management Plan that covers the approach to pesticide use in line with the FAO safeguard measures on pests and pesticides. This plan makes specific provision for the training of beneficiaries, as well as sectoral actor chiefs on the environment and technical aspects of integrated pest management. The applicable standard is FAO Environmental and Social Standard (ESS 5). The objectives of ESS 5 are the following: to ensure that the use of pesticides and other chemicals is minimized; to minimize the negative impact of pesticide use. In line with the explanation of the scope of ESS 5, this standard requires that the Project will not promote the use of pesticides or chemical fertilizers in agroforestry (particularly) and forestry production systems could indirectly contribute to an increase in the use of certain pesticides. The FAO must promote integrated pest management as a basic principle for sustainable

## Selected Risk Factor 9: Labor issues including child labor, discrimination in selection processes, and abusive working conditions may be linked to some project activities.

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Prohibited practices | Medium | Low |

### Description
Some labor abuses could be associated with the Project activities. Such instances could include for example, employment of children below 16 years of age in agriculture or other sectors, particularly in poorer remote locations where school attendance may not be regular and where families depend on child labor to supplement their income. Child labor is also a concern when it is used by contractors, particularly by subcontractors or project partners. For instance, women or indigenous peoples may face pressure from family members to work for low wages and poor conditions in order to provide additional benefits. Sexual harassment in the workplace or during implementation of project activities may also occur.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The Project will comply with FAO Environmental and Social Management Guidelines (Standard 7) and FAO’s General Principles on Child Labor, which are based on ILO Convention No. 138 and ILO Recommendation No. 146. The Project will also comply with the environmental and social policy standards of the FAF, the framework on ending child labor in agriculture. Incidence of child labor in project supported activities will lead to the immediate suspension of support to the implementation of such activities until the problem is resolved.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 67 OF 73

## Selected Risk Factor 11: The range of measures developed to reduce pressure on natural forests is restricted by this framework.

### Category
- Technical and operational
### Probability
- Low
### Impact
- Low

**Description**

The Project will educate stakeholders in the provinces and 13 districts on the importance of their and others' interests in natural forest conservation. Under Component 1 of the Project land owner families will be supported to adopt sustainable forest management practices, including the development and implementation of measures to reduce the need to clear or overuse natural forest lands. The Project aims to stabilize agriculture and increase the income from agricultural activities for local communities.

However, these measures do not increase the value of the forest and they do not create direct motivation for natural forest use by local populations. Current legal trends are increasingly oriented towards the effective involvement of indigenous peoples in forest management and conservation. The new Forest Code makes provisions for the involvement of local communities and indigenous populations in legislation related to forests, including land tenure.

The New Forest Code is still under construction. Article 37 of Law No. 43-2014 of October 10, 2014 on land-use planning and management provides that the State will not grant permits for any use of natural resources, including forests. The REDD+ National Strategy recommends the development of a legal framework to ensure the effective participation of local communities in forest management.

This option is not available at this time. Currently, there is no framework with specific laws and implementing legislation to increase the responsibility of local communities and women's control in forest breeding and use.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

An indicator has been created to monitor deforestation and forest degradation. The Project will raise awareness among indigenous people about their rights as owners of forests and their responsibilities for their forests, and will negotiate amendments with the MEF for increased local community responsibility for forest management. The Project will also provide training in sustainable forest management practices that are generally mixed and made up of Bantu and indigenous populations.

The Project will raise awareness of the local people on the future possibility of participatory natural forest management as an incentive to conserve the forests, and will encourage them to develop their own rules to control access and use of their local forests. The Project will advise communities about the rights they already enjoy.


# Selected Risk Factor 12: Limited collateral from beneficiaries may prevent access to micro-credit or may lead to delinquency.

### Category
- Other
### Probability
- Low
### Impact
- Low

**Description**

Besides indigenous people, whose investment cost will be covered at 100%, the Project will only cover part of the investment costs for other beneficiaries. The Project will provide micro-credit loans to a limited number of some categories of beneficiaries without collateral, regardless of their potential to successfully adopt agroforestry and forestry systems promoted by the Project. For beneficiaries who get access to micro-credit, there is a risk that they may be in a precarious financial situation when repaying the loan (generate the benefit margin needed to reimburse the loan).

## Mitigation Measure(s)

To mitigate the risk associated with limited collateral, the Project will exclusively target smallholder groups. Targeting smallholder groups will help by pooling necessary resources from individual group members to build consensus and ensure that all members are able to repay their loans. The extent of Project support will be subject, among other things, to the relevance of the financing strategy, including the

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 68 OF 73

**Selected Risk Factor 13: Institutional instability (political risks) may prevent timely implementation of Project activities**

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | Low |

Description

Several sectoral ministries will play an important role in the implementation of the Project as potential procured parties. Congo's administrative structure is characterized by decision-making power mostly concentrated in the hand of the minister or his deputy. Given the political nature of these positions, and considering the long duration of the Project (10 years), it is possible that political instability could prevent timely implementation of some Project activities.

Mitigation measures

Building on more than 40 years of presence in Congo, FAO has established and maintained different platforms for political dialogue with the Government of Congo at the highest decision-making level. FAO has consistently relied on these platforms to ensure a conducive environment for the smooth execution and timely implementation of its activities. In addition, the Project is cross-cutting by nature with decision-making power distributed across several sectors. The Project will therefore be implemented in such a way as to minimize interference in the implementation of overall Project activities without significant interference even in a context of political instability within one or more sectoral ministries.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## D.1. Environment and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

A mission to identify and analyze environmental and social risks was conducted between May 21 and June 8, 2019 in the main districts and villages targeted by the Project, as a supplement to the previously conducted research necessary for the preparation of the Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS). The research was carried out by CERCAP in the three Project area districts. The visited districts extended from the extreme north of the Project area (Chiriquí District) to the extreme south (Darién District).

The risk category assigned to this Project is Category B — corresponding to activities with limited adverse environmental and social impacts. This categorization is also consistent with the internal categorization conducted by the FAO during conceptual design, which identified the Project as having a low environmental and social risk profile due to three main factors: (a) The project area is dominated by savanna with a relatively low biodiversity value — anthrax savanna vegetation type; (b) The project area has no significant cultural or religious sites of importance; (c) The project area contains natural forests with a high biodiversity value. (d) The Project is designed to overcome non-equitable aspects in the current land access system, which are not related to the Project itself and will be addressed by the Government in the medium- and long-term. However, there is a risk that certain strata of non-land owners, in particular women, young people and indigenous peoples, will not be able to benefit from land access in the same way as men.

The Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) for the Project was developed based on the findings of the environmental and social risk assessment. The ESMS is built upon the environmental and social safeguards established by the World Bank Group (WBG), the International Finance Corporation (IFC), and the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). It also builds on the REDD+ Principles, Criteria, Indicators, and Guidelines for Forest Carbon Projects. The ESMS is based on three main components: (a) Strategic Environmental and Social Assessment (SESA); (b) Project-level environmental and social management; and (c) Monitoring and evaluation of project-level environmental and social performance.

The SESA will be conducted by the Government in collaboration with CERCAP, and will cover all aspects related to the Project. The Project-level ESMS will be developed for each Component Activity and will include a description of the environmental and social risks associated with that activity, as well as mitigation measures. The monitoring and evaluation methods will be identified. No resettlement of populations is planned. A complaints management system will operate at all levels of the project.

Some indigenous populations were identified in the district of Ngó during the feasibility study mission. The Project focuses on the communities of Ngó, Chiriquí District, which are located within the boundaries of the Project area and have a high level of social organization. The Project team conducted surveys among these people to identify their main concerns related to land access, agricultural production methods, food security, livelihoods, relations with the Bantu populations, internal hierarchy, land access methods, experiences with agricultural producer groups. They were informed about the Project Interventions as well as their participation and specific concerns.

The Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) was developed for implementation. During the risk analysis, eight main risks were identified (as presented in Table 1). These risks are related to: (a) The project area is dominated by savanna with a relatively low biodiversity value — anthrax savanna vegetation type; (b) The project area has no significant cultural or religious sites of importance; (c) The project area contains natural forests with a high biodiversity value. (d) The Project is designed to overcome non-equitable aspects in the current land access system, which are not related to the Project itself and will be addressed by the Government in the medium- and long-term.

Among the production systems under consideration, the cocoa-based systems risk becomes less profitable to the extent that the project area is dominated by savanna with a relatively low biodiversity value. This is because cocoa trees require more water than other crops (main impact). To minimize this risk, the Project will ensure, in return, that the promoted activities, including cocoa growing by beneficiaries, will not exceed 10 hectares and that the selected land will be not classified as natural forest or permanent wetlands. The project area is dominated by savanna with a relatively low biodiversity value (main impact). To minimize this risk, the Project will ensure that the promoted activities, including cocoa growing by beneficiaries, will not exceed 10 hectares and that the selected land will be not classified as natural forest or permanent wetlands.

The minimum plantation size for formal groups makes minimum access to the best qualified agricultural lands difficult. This is because the project area is dominated by savanna with a relatively low biodiversity value (main impact). To minimize this risk, the Project will ensure that the promoted activities, including cocoa growing by beneficiaries, will not exceed 10 hectares and that the selected land will be not classified as natural forest or permanent wetlands.

The quality of governance by government institutions is considered to be moderate. This is because the project area is dominated by savanna with a relatively low biodiversity value (main impact). To minimize this risk, the Project will ensure that the promoted activities, including cocoa growing by beneficiaries, will not exceed 10 hectares and that the selected land will be not classified as natural forest or permanent wetlands.

The ESMS will be implemented in accordance with the procedures established for the project and on the previous documents at district, project and GCF level.

## (NAA) The interviewed indigenous populations generally felt incapable of negotiating usage contracts with land owners to plant trees and believed that this would be possible with the support of the Project and the government. They all declared their interest in taking part in the Project, and a workshop to confirm this wish was held during the Project development phase.

The team found that indigenous populations are victims of much discrimination by Bantu populations. Without the Project's support, they will not be able to participate effectively in the project activities or to defend themselves with respect of their civil rights and increase their ability to fully benefit from the Project's support were defined. A report of this meeting was prepared and signed by all adults present. A second mission was scheduled to provide further sensitization on the importance of indigenous populations' participation, as well as to inform them about the project and to raise their awareness of Law No. 5/2011 of February 25, 2011 relating to the promotion and protection of the rights of indigenous peoples in the Democratic Republic of Congo (REPARIC). This mission was organized by the National Association for the Promotion and Protection of Indigenous Peoples of Congo (RENAPAC). During this mission, a consultative meeting was organized during which the chiefs of 13 villages were present. The meeting was held on February 28th.

Finally, a road map was prepared containing the steps needed to prepare a Planning Framework for Indigenous Peoples and within this framework, it is recommended that key measures would be the requirement that all opportunities offered by the Bank should also be offered to the indigenous populations.

## G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The socio-legal status of women in Congo remains a major concern. Although the 2013 Constitution enshrines the principle that all citizens are equal before the law, it does not provide any specific measures to convey this equality.

In order to identify any gender disparities that could prevent women from taking part in and fully enjoying the benefits of the project activities, a gender assessment was conducted. The assessment was carried out by the GCF team in order to conduct a situational analysis in order to guarantee compliance of the practical needs and strategic interests of both men and women. The assessment was conducted with the participation of 100 women from the provinces of Nari, Bouenza, Pool and Plateaux. A total of approximately 20 discussion groups were organized with women's groups, as well as with local service providers. The situational analysis was conducted following the guidelines of Agence de la Gestion des Concessions (AGC) and the Gender Action Plan (GAP). The assessment highlighted that the assessment allowed the disparities and social constraints faced by women within the context of agricultural and forestry production.

In terms of cultural barriers, the following can be noted: the inequality of land access and security rights under customary law; the lack of education for women in rural areas; the low level of literacy among women; their limited ability to convey their views in the community as well as within mixed agricultural groups; poor access to information, coupled with low representation at public meetings and consultations, thus limiting their full participation in public decision-making at community level.

Obstacles related to education were also raised, characterized by high levels of illiteracy within women's agricultural production groups. This is due to the fact that women are not able to read or write, which limits their access to opportunities on agricultural techniques and other capacity building sessions that may be offered to them.

In terms of socio-economic obstacles or disparities, the assessment highlighted the financial vulnerability of women from certain communities in relation to the lack of resources available for investment. This is due to the small size of their crop yields and the fact that they devote nearly all of their resources from their production to family expenses, making it difficult for them to invest.

The assessment also highlighted unmet needs for training and technical support related to agriculture and forestry activities for women in groups, with consequences negatively impacting their productivity. There are major obstacles which prevent women from participating fully in the project's activities: lack of access to information on sustainable and profitable practices.

Despite these challenges, the beneficiaries showed that access by women to information in villages is largely dependent on the channel whereby this information is distributed. For this reason, the Project is placing special emphasis on ensuring that women have access to information through a variety of channels, not only so that women not only have timely access to information, but also that they can take part in meetings and public consultations. To this end, the agricultural sector chiefs will be deployed as intermediaries in the villages.

### G.3 Financial Management and Procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

In terms of purchases/acquisitions, fiduciary responsibility will be assumed by FAO effectively and professionally and in accordance with standards and procedures as set out in the General Conditions of Employment Agreement (GCEA) with the GCF.

As indicated above, FAO will enter into the Administrative Entity (AE) and financial agency contracts with the GCF and will therefore be responsible for the implementation of Project funds, as well as accounting and provision of information on the use of funds. Further to approval of the Project by the GCF, FAO will disseminate its Operations Manual and Procurement Procedure Manual to all relevant stakeholders involved in the project. This includes the members of the Technical Committee and other relevant stakeholders prior to the start of the Project's activities. During implementation, the PMI will be responsible for ensuring that the Project is implemented according to the approved strategic planning, technical control, financial management and procurement. Finally, the PMI will be first in line to determine whether the project has been implemented as per the approved strategic plan, with the assistance of personnel assigned by the government (MET and MAEP), including a gender expert and a certified public accountant.

The procurement and financial management standards will comply with FAO standards and procedures in alignment with GCF policies. The PMI will be responsible for ensuring that all procurements are conducted in accordance with the Technical Officer (TTO) who will conduct a monitoring and evaluation mission twice a year to ensure effective implementation of the Project, including compliance with the approved strategic plan and financial and budgetary monitoring. This monitoring and evaluation report will be presented to the SC during biannual meetings.

The acquisition of goods or services necessary for Project implementation, training, consultation, as well as financial transactions will be carried out by FAO in accordance with the GCF's General Conditions of Employment Agreement (GCEA) and the Procurement Procedure Manual. All procurement will be carried out by the PMI. As previously indicated, FAO will be the main Executing Entity (EE) for the project, and will implement the Project in close collaboration with the MET and MAEP.
