# Secretariat’s assessment of FP159

## Proposal name: PREFOREST CONGO - Project to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from forests in five departments in the Republic of Congo

### Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

### Country/[ies]: Republic of Congo

### Project/programme size: Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The project targets key food- and fuelwood-producing areas that sustain the major urban centres in the Congo, and has the opportunity to contribute significantly towards very sustainable and environmentally friendly production zones. | The sustainability plan needs to be developed to ensure capture of carbon emissions. |
| The various technical and investment platforms provide a unique opportunity to standardize and scale up the project’s impact on scalability in the targeted zones and beyond, focusing on creating investment opportunities for smallholders in the target zones. | Financial institutions, business opportunities and new modalities need to be explored, and incentives for adoption by farmers need to be analyzed before a second disbursement. |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum XVI, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations."

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background


---
**Page 84**
---

1. **FORESTED Congo - Project to Reduce Greenhouse Gas Emissions from Forests in Five Departments of the Republic of the Congo** aims to reduce carbon emissions through three deforestation and forest degradation hotspots in the south of the country, where deforestation and forest degradation are most severe.
2. The project will contribute to direct carbon emissions reductions of R$8,485 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2eq) per year, resulting in 6.71 MLOeQ over the 30-year period of project implementation. This is achieved through a combination of measures including deployment of agroforestry systems, assisted natural regeneration and protection of forests.
3. The project will also contribute to reducing vulnerability by increasing the adaptive capacity of about 41,373 direct beneficiaries (55 per cent women) in five departments of the Republic of Congo. This includes small farmers, women, young people and indigenous peoples among others.
4. These five departments are Bouenza, Kouilou, Niari, Plateaux and Pool. These feed the major urban areas such as Brazzaville and Pointe-Noire, as well as the capital city of Kinshasa in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC). The five departments accounted for the highest increase in deforestation and forest degradation from 2012-2016.
5. The main drivers of deforestation and forest degradation are slash-and-burn agriculture, logging, mining and urbanization. The project will support local communities to engage in slash-and-burn and short rotations, preventing long fallow periods that encourage further deforestation.

The total project financing needed is US$ 46,567,138 with a request to GCF for grant financing of US$ 20,980,852. The Government, through the Ministry of Forest Economy (MEF), is providing co-financing in the amount of US$ 15,586,286. The Global Environment Facility (GEF) is supporting with a grant of USD 7 million and the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) is contributing with a loan of USD 14.1 million. The project is submitted as an environmental and social safeguards (ESS) category 5.

### Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Land-use and resource planning and strengthening of land access and security rights
(total cost US$ 0.9 million; GCF cost US$ 0.18 million or 20 per cent)

- **Component 1** will establish the critical enabling environment prerequisites to achieve the project’s objectives by providing a legal framework, strengthening land access and security rights for the project beneficiaries.
- The component will carry out participatory mapping of land use and tenure in the five departments. This is deemed appropriate to identify and divide different land uses to set the agroforestry systems (component 2) and to establish a monitoring reporting verification system of the Congo in order to completely align methodologies and indicators with the national forest reference emission level (FREL). This has been assessed as feasible.
- The second part of the component will secure access to about 15,000 ha of land for selected communities through the implementation of customary tenure agreements or land use agreements. The proposed selection criteria are deemed appropriate, although detailed final criteria should be submitted prior to implementation of activities 2.1 and 2.2. The greatest strength is that it will contribute directly to the achievement of the Paris Agreement National Programme (PNRAB), which is key to achieving the outcomes of the proposed activities.
- The project’s support for securing tenure rights, shared resumeration systems - have been assessed in the feasibility study as being viable.


---
**Page 85**
---

9\. The project prioritizes vulnerable populations of the target areas by aiming to secure at least 30 per cent of the planned land access (i.e., 5,000 ha) for the most marginalized people, including women and indigenous peoples. The project should adhere to equity and ensure equal opportunities for all groups in accessing land, especially for women, youth, and other lands as well for land access and tenure rights.

16\. This component is to be 100 per cent financed by GCF grants. In order to ensure ownership of the assets from the proposed activities, it is advised to seek co-financing opportunities as a way of increasing country ownership of the project activities.

**Component 2: Establishment of agroforestry and forestry systems for climate change mitigation (USD 105 million; GCF USD 83.9 million or 41 per cent)**

1\. Component 2 will establish agroforestry and forestry systems on a total of 14,500 ha of degraded land in the project area using a mixed species planting approach and the agroforestry method. The project will use fast-growing tree species such as acacia species for sustainable fuelwood supply. The proposal provides indications of the environmental impacts of using such fast-growing trees on soil fertility and water quality, which are expected to be minimal.

2\. It is expected that beneficiary farmers would be consulted on species selection prior to their planting in the project area.

12\. The component will mix fast-growing trees with other staple and cash crops depending on different forest areas. The agroforestry models will be determined based on certain criteria that will ensure that the proposed systems are economically viable, socially acceptable, and environmentally suitable for use by smallholder farmers. This component will be in conjunction with the creation of the enabling environment to support the development of a market for tree products.

13\. ### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential
**Scale: High**

- The project has significant impact potential as a mitigation project with 6.72 MTCO₂eq over the period of project implementation, or 16.7 MtCO₂eq over the lifetime of the project's lifespan. These reductions are mainly to be achieved through the sustainable transformation of the production landscapes supplying the urban areas with food and fodder, through the promotion of agroforestry systems, reducing deforestation and forest degradation, slash-and-burn agriculture, and reducing pressure on forests. These factors are the major drivers of GHG emissions in the project area.
- These important productive basins will be transformed, and provide a new basis for rural poor communities to diversify their livelihood strategies. The project will have substantial adaptation co-benefits (e.g., 10 per cent of the total population is estimated to be food insecure; 25 per cent of them women) and $780.4 M in indirect beneficiaries through reduced vulnerability and increased resilience, as well as improved access to markets and agroforestry systems and forest capacity-building activities from the proposed project.
- The project's carbon sequestration and avoided emissions benefits were calculated using the IPCC 2013 methodology and are consistent with the best guidelines of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change for national GCF investments as well as the national FREL of the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC). These estimates have been validated by the national forest monitoring systems and FREL to continue to be improved and updated.

#### 3.2 Paradigm-shift potential
**Scale: High**

- The project will help restore and conserve key production landscapes in the Congo, and through the innovation of the technical and investment platforms, contribute to long-term transformation and scalability to other regions in the Congo and surrounding countries.
- The project is a paradigm shift for rural development in the DRC. It offers a very innovative approach through first restoring landscapes to offer multiple opportunities and livelihood diversification options for many rural poor communities; and by creating long-term change, with the potential of generating new markets and creating opportunities for local entrepreneurship.
- These areas are the key production landscapes in the Congo, supplying millions of residents in the majority of urban areas with food, fodder and fuel. This is important as the transition in urban areas to natural gas for cooking is predicted to be a lengthy process. The project can contribute significantly by providing an alternative energy source that will allow several areas to a more sustainable and green system, while in parallel growing and expanding market opportunities for rural poor communities. It also provides an opportunity to increase investments and focus on local rural innovation and business ideas, which will not


---
**Page 87**
---

3.3 Sustainable development potential

- The project focuses on communities as the centerpiece and, through the platforms initiated by the Green Climate Fund, the communities will be at the center of the project to create jobs and stimulate growth in rural areas while improving the management of landscapes and ecosystems.
- The economic, social and environmental co-benefits are high and long-term oriented.

3.4 Needs of the recipients

- The project targets the departments with the highest levels of deforestation and forest degradation and those with communities living at high poverty levels. It will therefore give priority to improve the high reliance on fuelwood and improve its consumption, while restoring and managing the production landscapes better and providing key opportunities to poor communities.
- The project seeks to transform local livelihood strategies by improving the enabling environment for sustainable land management practices and agroforestry systems in rural areas which will help transform the local rural areas for good. The various platforms to be launched by the project will also play a key role for communities engaged in food production, cash and overall business development with long-term support, transformation and job creation.

3.5 Country ownership

- Consistent with the high level of ambition set forward by the Government prioritizing energy efficiency and targeting unsustainable fuelwood harvesting, and to mention its national REED strategy.
- The project is aligned with key forest, agriculture and energy policies and strategies in the country.
- The project offers a unique opportunity to implement key policy in alignment with one another, such as the agriculture, forestry and energy sectors, offering an innovative approach for combined land use management. This will allow for replication in many other areas of the country, as well as provide important platforms on which to build new, future-focused initiatives.


---
**Page 88**
---

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The project is assessed to be highly efficient, costing USD 243/(€0.5) with the overall cost and benefits ratio being GCF grantable. Financial viability is determined by a financial model with multiple agroforestry models that shows positive net present values (NPV13 different values depending on the agroforestry models) with GCF grants. The assumption of NPV13 is that the project will be able to generate revenues that are positive enough to sustain project activities after the completion of the project. Economic NPV is also substantial, at USD 240/(€0.5), which represents a return on investment (ROI) of 14.4 per cent.

The requested financial instruments (i.e., GCF grants) appear as being adequate. Credit and banking services in the Congo are currently either not available or affordable for smallholder farmers. Agriculture banks are also non-existent in the Congo. GCF grants are derived from a combination of public and private sector financing, including microfinance institutions, to support smallholder agriculture in the target areas for smallholder farmers.

The project will leverage co-financing from the Government of the Congo, CAFI and IFAD with a credit line provided by the World Bank GCF and co-financers. The concessional loan from IFAD is expected to remove risks and barriers in accessing markets, and is expected to motivate financial institutions to provide additional financing for smallholder farmers.

The financing from CAFI will create sustainable platforms for local entrepreneurship to thrive and grow.


### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

Environmental and social risk category and safeguards instruments. The project aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by reducing the rate of deforestation and forest degradation, which are major sources of carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions in the communities of the local people. It plans to accomplish this by addressing the main drivers of deforestation and degradation, which are slash-and-burn agriculture, and unsustainable production and consumption patterns. The project will address these issues through: (1) land resources planning, and strengthening of land access and security rights; (2) deployment of low-emission climate-smart agricultural practices that improve soil fertility and water retention, increase sustainable supply; and (4) strengthening national agricultural financing structures, business capacity building, and the development of a rural finance platform. The project will also address other environmental risks such as biodiversity loss in areas like Bouenza, Kouilou, Nana, Plateaux and Pool where deforestation and degradation rates are highest.

The Secretariat confirms this category gives the scale, approach and main objectives of the interventions. The individual activities on the ground are aimed at community-based and mainly gender-focused approaches to addressing the drivers of forest degradation. The approach also puts heavy emphasis on community participation and establishing a strong governance structure through the establishment of an environmental management framework (EMF), including an indigenous peoples planning framework.


#### 5 Key environmental, social risks and impacts and management

The following are key environmental and social risks to GCF funding that may be addressed in the proposal.

ESMIF - Assessment and evaluation of critical and significant environmental risks and impacts. The project proponents have developed an ESMIF that: (i) provides a general assessment of the risks associated with the interventions under the project; (ii) describes the


---
**Page 89**
---

process of planning and managing the risks and impacts of individual project activities (subprojects); and (iii) provides a governance structure for the implementation of safeguards during project implementation. All subprojects will be subjected to environmental and social screening in accordance with the Environmental and Social Management Framework of the United Nations (ESMF). Depending on the results of the screening, the subprojects will undergo further assessment in accordance with the country's environmental impact assessment system.

### ESS 2 - Labour and working conditions. The project management unit may need to hire staff for implementation of the project activities. These activities include: planting micro- to medium-scale agroforestry/forestry systems, and the purchase and installation of storage facilities. Depending on the scale of these activities, the project management unit likely will involve the hiring of workers. The ESMRF provides that an assessment of labour and working conditions in relation to project activities will be carried out during the project inception phase. This includes a review of existing laws and regulations regarding labour and the hiring of miners in the project areas, and prepare labour management and occupational health and safety plans.

### ESS 3 - Resource efficiency and pollution prevention. The project is expected to result in some minor and localized pollution due to emissions and wastewater discharges from storage facilities and processing units. To address this risk, the project has incorporated identified risks in the use of pesticides in agroforestry farms to be supported by the project, and has incorporated measures for reducing air pollution from the burning of biomass, preventing air and water pollution from storage, and from processing facilities of agroforestry products, will be addressed during the project inception phase.

### ESS 4 - Community health, safety and security. The project agroforestry/forestry initiatives assisted natural regeneration and implementation of the various plans could significantly reduce the incidence of diseases in the community. This is because there has been no or emergence of latent diseases due to changes in the ecosystem. This is being identified in the ESMRF. However, there are risks associated with the project activities that need to be addressed. There is no existing data as to the presence and prevalence of diseases in the prospective project sites; therefore, it will not be possible to identify specific disease risks or potential outbreaks (e.g., vector-borne diseases) as a result of reforestation. In this regard, there may be a need to adopt a single disease surveillance and alert system in the project areas during project implementation.

### ESS 5 - Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement. The planning activities in components one and two will involve land use/area access restrictions for the project phase; this will involve demarcation of conservation areas and assisted natural regeneration in savannah-forest transition. This demarcation or classification of areas would likely entail land-use/access restrictions, which could result in involuntary resettlement of communities that have no documented claims on both public and private lands. Component 2 activities, particularly the establishment of agroforestry farms, will involve land use/area access restrictions for potential voluntary/involuntary resettlement impacts due to possible displacement of informal settlers. The project has incorporated measures to address these risks through the restriction of access to forest resources, affecting traditional forest users. This risk will be avoided through strict requirements on the use of only those lands that are free from claims by other beneficiaries other than the beneficiaries from the programme support. The project can ensure this risk is avoided by including it in the project's environmental and social screening form.

### ESS 6 - Biodiversity. The project activities may result in adverse impacts to existing natural resources. The project is expected to improve biodiversity and is meant a measure for conservation of natural resources. However, if not properly managed, reforestation and afforestation efforts may result in the inadvertent introduction of new and invasive species of flora and fauna and thus, reduce biodiversity. The ESMRF has adequately assessed these risks.


---
**Page 90**
---

and mitigation measures have already been incorporated in the project design. For example, to prevent expansion of agroforestry farms into forest areas, agroforestry systems will only be deployed strictly within a context of local development plans and fuelwood supply master plans in accordance with the REEDS-plus programme. The project area is characterized by its low biodiversity; it has only five hectares in forest areas. Within the technical committee, the project will create a biodiversity monitoring plan to ensure that the project does not have an adverse impact on biodiversity. In terms of introduction of invasive species, the subcommittee will screen out species for use in agroforestry and natural regeneration measures.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy

The project proposes to ensure that there could be around ten villages in the project area that may need attention under the indigenous peoples policy and an indigenous peoples planning framework has been prepared. The following sections have been prepared to outline the following project risks relating to indigenous peoples:

- **Risk of indigenous peoples being excluded from the project:** The indigenous peoples are among the most vulnerable groups affected by climate change, and they face the risk of continued marginalization of the indigenous peoples, and the high probability that they will not benefit proportionally from the project. In general, the project will favour the most marginalized communities in terms of access to resources and services provided by the state lands. Indigenous peoples will also benefit from targeted capacity-building, as well as technical assistance for land tenure rights.

- **It is particularly important for the percentage of indigenous peoples benefiting from the project to be proportional to the number of Bantu in the village benefiting from the project:** Moreover, the project will ensure that indigenous peoples are not excluded from benefits, particularly through training and support in securing their land tenure rights, as well as the establishment of a grievance redress mechanism.

- **Risks of rights of indigenous peoples not being respected:** The project may include areas considered traditional homes of indigenous peoples, especially in the departments of Nariño and Putumayo. These areas are also home to indigenous communities such as the Emberá, who live on land known as Nilo District. An indigenous peoples planning framework has been prepared.

- **The project is not expected to have impacts on cultural heritage:** The ESMR will be conducted in accordance with sacred grounds, religious buildings, and burial sites are to be avoided and excluded from the ground intervention activities. This will be verified through a detailed review of the project’s environmental and social screening form.

### Stakeholder engagement and grievance redress mechanism (GMR)

The project proposes to engage stakeholders during the stakeholder consultations, which is a standard requirement under the REEDS-plus programme. The AF has submitted a stakeholder consultation report that describes the process used for engaging with stakeholders in the project area. The stakeholder engagement plan per se, plans and strategies for continued stakeholder engagement during implementation of the project will be developed as part of the overall project management proposal. For instance, one of the outputs of the project includes the development and implementation of a communication strategy to engage with key stakeholders (e.g., community leaders, opinion leaders, decision makers, etc.) in the target areas through various platforms. These platforms include media activities in the form of television, radio and press advertisements,

### Gender policy

The AF has provided a gender assessment and a gender action plan (GAP), and therefore complied with the requirements of the Gender Policy of Gabon.

The Republic of Congo has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women in 1985. The country also signed the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights, which promotes the rights of women. The Constitution of the Republic of the Congo (2005) assigns women the same rights as men with respect to marriage, divorce, inheritance, and property.

It also commits to taking all necessary measures to eliminate violence against women. Congolese law requires that at least 30% of candidates for public office be women. The law provides a minimum requirement of 15 per cent in terms of the proportion of women candidates for elected positions in local government bodies. In addition, the Ministry of Women’s Affairs and promoting women's empowerment is the Ministry for the Promotion of Women and the Integration of Women in Development. Validated in November 2014, the National Gender Policy aims to achieve gender equality by 2035 through a series of measures aimed at improving the status and rights of girls and women in the Congo.

While formal enabling environment does exist, it is often at odds with the Congo’s traditional law and customs. Traditionally, Congolese society is largely organized in a patriarchal system. Traditional laws, custom, and some religious practices support discrimination against women. For example, under the Customary Code, giving the rights of spouses in marriage, leaves decision-making power largely in the hands of men. Violence against women is also widespread. The lack of awareness about their legal rights/processes has many women with land rights, access to finance, and income, productive assets, and property. This is due to the fact that women have a limited awareness of national-level land laws and the guarantees that they could afford women. Poverty levels among women are higher than those for men. Women-headed households live in poverty. Access to social services is limited for women (especially in rural areas), and women’s access to education, health care, and employment opportunities at all levels of girls and women continue to lag those of boys and men and continue to contribute to the vulnerability of women to climate impacts.

The gender assessment was based on desk reviews and consultations with various stakeholders. Gender issues highlight gender issues in the forestry and agriculture sectors. In these sectors, the roles of women and men tend to follow a gender-based division of labour. In terms of forest management, women are responsible for collecting firewood, harvesting non-timber forest products, and fuelwood, as well as engaging in activities related to water collection and energy needs. Men


---
**Page 92**
---

mainly for forest for commercial purposes, such as housing/bushmeat, charcoal and timber. In the geographical areas that are the focus of the project, agriculture is mainly characterised by subsistence food crop production dominated by polyculture. Picking practices use traditional methods, including slash-and-burn (swidden) farming systems. Women play a major role in pre-season activities (e.g., preparing fields and planting), during the growing season (e.g., weeding and harvesting), post-harvest processing and marketing agricultural products. In addition, women cultivate home gardens that provide food for their families as well as cash crops to meet household needs of the family (food, school fees, clothing, health costs etc.). Whether married or single, women are responsible for most of the domestic work in the households and operations of the family. This makes it difficult for women to save any money to expand agricultural production. Women also have limited access to credit and other financial services in the agriculture sector and use of forest resources; women continue to have limited access to training, extension services and decision-making positions within the forestry sector due to low literacy rates among women and by the heavy workload and time poverty they face. Women's participation in local government and decision-making in communities is also low. This is also due to lack of awareness about their rights as well as inadequate capacity building for women leaders and the limited resources and institutional commitment allocated to implementing existing laws and policies.

54. The AF has responded, as per the requirements of the Gender Policy, a GAP that includes activities, targets, indicators, budget, timeline and gender expertise to support implementation. The activities are designed to address the key challenges and barriers women face in agriculture; some of the key challenges and barriers women face in the sector. The GAP includes associated indicators and targets for monitoring and evaluation of project activities. This is within the AF's responsibility for the implementation of GAP activities. The AF will provide gender experts to ensure that the project addresses the identified gender-related issues, including planning, monitoring and evaluation of project activities. This support will be bolstered through contracted services from the Gender Unit of the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and the Integration of Women in Development. The associated activities of the GAP include technical capacity-building for women in sustainable agricultural practices, agroforestry, and financial management skills; gender mainstreaming throughout the project implementation and monitoring and evaluation processes; and awareness raising on gender equality and income-generating activities for women. These activities will be complemented with building awareness among men about their roles as partners in family life and in achieving gender-related objectives and associated activities that are linked to: strengthening the adoption of more sustainable agricultural practices, increasing access to credit and other financial services and financial capacities of producers; and an activity dedicated to strengthening the technical and institutional capacities of the project team on the gender dimension. Corresponding targets are included for each of these activities. The AF will also ensure that women's participation increases across the project, with aspirations for equal participation, including in decision-making and planning processes. The AF will take measures to reduce the risk of gender-based violence (GBV) and will take measures to mitigate these risks and ensure women have access to grievance mechanisms. This will be achieved through a GBV risk assessment included in the project's communication strategy; by encouraging couples' dialogue, and by including GBV in the project's monitoring and evaluation framework. The AF will also provide technical support in support in addressing GBV. The GAP also includes activities designed to collect lessons learned related to the successes and failures of reaching its objectives.

### Risks

#### 4.3 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

The project aims to reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation caused by slash-and-burn agriculture, unsustainable land use practices, and consumption of fuelwood through several interventions: (i) land-use planning and strengthening of land access and security rights; (ii) establishment of agroforestry and forestry systems for climate change mitigation and adaptation in the agricultural sector; and (iii) development of sustainable forest management and forestry sectors. The total project cost is projected to be USD 46.6 million, of which USD 29 million will be financed by the Green Climate Fund (GCF) (USD 10 million grant), IFAD (USD 1.6 million senior loan), and the MEF (USD 9 million in-kind).

FAO, as the AE, will act as the executing entity (EE) for all GCF- and GAF-funded activities. Co-financing related to IFAD will be executed by the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries (MAEP).

#### 6.2 Accredited entity/executing entity's capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

FAO is the AE and EE for GCF funds. FAO has a track record in implementing agricultural and climate resilience projects, and has over 40 years' presence in the Congo. FAO has recently carried out similar activities in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) and Burundi, and has a track record in coconuts and woody energy projects. An AE National entity will undertake most of the implementation.

The AE is also implementing similar projects with technical and financial support of development partners. This includes a USD 100 million project for development of commercial agriculture to be implemented from 2018 to 2022. Additional comfort is derived from recent experience in accordance with FAO procedures, and the creation of a ringfenced implementation unit.

#### Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

Strength of implementing parties: National entities will undertake most of the implementation activities under the supervision of FAO in accordance with FAO procurement rules. The quality of the parties selected, and their continued financial viability and capability, will determine the success of project implementation.

Land use: Many landowners do not have a formal land title. The adoption of climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry production systems will specifically target non-landowners. In order to secure long-term tenure for these activities, it is necessary that non-landowners, or even agree to conclude long-term shared remuneration contracts with non-landowners. This may be difficult in practice as the rights of land users are not always secured with land titles. Social conflicts may also give rise to speculation with respect to the monetary value of rural land.

Project-specific governance risks: The AE has identified political and governance aspects of the technical and administrative governments as risks for project execution. Comfort is derived


---
**Page 94**
---

from the implementation arrangements via the PMU with involvement of the AE as well as the long-term presence of the AE in the region.

### Project viability and concessionality

64. Project EERs are high at 63.4 per cent over a period of 20 years, taking into account the value of reduced GHG emissions. The financial NPV of the climate-resilient forestry systems are strong for all project components (Component 1: $58.7 million; Component 2: $90.0 million; and Component 3: $100.8 million) over a period of 20 years without grant use), suggesting potential for partial or reimbursable grants. The AE considers that grant use is appropriate given the country context and regional experience with climate-resilient forestry systems.

71. However, FAO advises whether there will be any disbursement or distribution of funds directly to entities, vouchers or other forms of direct payment to entity or individual beneficiaries as part of any of the activities of the project.

As a mitigation project, the funding proposal aims to mitigate 0.84 MtCO2eq of GHG emissions from deforestation and forest degradation over the lifetime of the project. It is expected to benefit 569,872 males and 14,480 females directly, and 565,822 and 304,727 females indirectly.

Overall, the funding proposal and logframe have been found to adequately apply the results management framework and performance measurement framework indicators. # Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP159

## Proposal name: PREFOREST CONGO - Project to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from forests in five departments in the Republic of Congo

### Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

### Country/ies: Republic of Congo

### Project/programme size: Small

## Assessment of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential Scale: Medium

#### 1.1. Impact potential
1. The Republic of Congo (the Congo), with a population of 5.2 million people and an area of 342,000 km², is located on the west coast of Central Africa.
2. The country is home to a vast forest area estimated at 23 million ha (69.8 per cent of the country's total land area), including 550,000 ha of protected areas and 14,000 km² of marine reserves.
3. The Congo has lost about 145,000 ha of forest in the period 2000–2012, one of the lowest rates in the Congo Basin.
4. The national economy is heavily dependent on the oil sector, as the fourth largest oil producer in Africa and the second largest exporter of crude oil in sub-Saharan Africa. However, political and economic instability. However, economic growth has slowed considerably since the past 2011.
5. Slash-and-burn farming and unsustainable production and consumption of firewood are the main drivers of deforestation and forest degradation in the Congo. About 80 per cent of the Congolese population depends on firewood for cooking and heating. The increase in deforestation and forest degradation is more pronounced in the south of the country.
6. The project proposes to implement a series of interventions around three components:
   - Component I will lay the groundwork for other activities by focusing on the securing of land access for small producers, participatory mapping, and research into agroforestry systems.
   - The project will also support the implementation of the National Afforestation and Reforestation Programme (Programme National d'Afforestation et de Réboisement – PRONARE) or land rehabilitation activities in five departments. These activities aim to improve forest management climate resilient agroforestry or forestry activities and will develop a shared reforestation system with stakeholders.


---
**Page 99**
---

1. Component 2 will deploy low-emission, climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry systems on micro and small initiatives implemented by community stakeholders that aim to stabilize slash-and-burn agriculture, increase resilience, and provide a sustainable fuelwood supply for households in the project area. These systems are based on agroforestry alternatives on natural forests, and an increase in smallholder farmers' adaptive capacity with agroforestry alternatives will be achieved through training and technical assistance. The initiative calls for sustainable fuelwood supply from resources from CAFL and 11,800 ha of micro and small forest-agroforestry and forestry systems (5-50 ha on average) in 13 target districts.
2. Under Component 2, the project will also support the development and upgrading of existing infrastructure in order to provide a sustainable source of high-quality plant materials for the establishment of agroforestry and forestry systems. This will be achieved through the implementation of 5,000 ha of assisted natural regeneration activities in areas already degraded by slash-and-burn.
3. Component 3 will seek to strengthen national agricultural financing structures, business capacities and value chains. The project will establish partnerships with the IFAD-supported National Forest Service (SINAFOR) and other public and private sector actors, including emerging micro, small and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs) in: (i) developing comprehensive and sustainable business plans; (ii) providing technical advisory services ranging from seedling production to marketing of products; and (iii) supporting the approved business plans under a shared cost-financing mechanism. The project will identify suitable credit lines for agroforestry and forestry projects, including microfinance institutions, commercial financial products and services for agrovalue chains. This component will also facilitate access to markets through the development of value chains and the establishment of networks of purchase agreements and sales platforms with partner institutions.
4. The mitigation estimates for the climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry models were made using a methodology developed by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO). The project will contribute to direct carbon emissions reductions of 1,702,563 tCO2e over eight years of project implementation or 4,872,970 tCO2e over the twenty years of the project.
5. The project will also have adaptation co-benefits, reducing the vulnerability and increasing the adaptive capacity of about 44,173 direct beneficiaries (14,486 of them women) and 170,000 indirect beneficiaries (29,000 of them women), including smallholder farmers and indigenous populations. The project claims that by disseminating climate-resilient agroforestry systems, the targeted communities will be able to diversify their livelihoods and incomes, thereby reducing their vulnerability to climate change. However, in general, the numbers of direct and indirect beneficiaries seem low in relation to the overall number of hectares expected.
6. The project does not include a detailed operational plan centred around the ecosystem-based adaptation approach. This is reflected by the lack of information on the overall number of hectares to be expected. They also explain that the project will follow an integrated landscape approach framed around the multifunctionality of the forest ecosystems, which includes the conservation and sustainable use of forests for multiple agriculture production and higher productivity, but also sustainable wood energy production and consumption. However, the project lacks specificity and details on the way to achieve this goal.

<sup>1</sup> Indirect beneficiaries have been estimated by taking into consideration those who will benefit indirectly from the establishment of the local participatory mapping in 13 districts.


---
**Page 100**
---

13. The project will start by carrying out a mapping of the current land use to identify priority sites with consideration of a landscape approach, and also the existence of buffer zones for potential expansion of deforestation and forest degradation. However, it seems that this should be done in conjunction with other projects.
14. As the establishment of fast-growing tree species to supply fuelwood as the major energy source for urban dwellers will take at least 7–8 years, the project proponents would like to identify alternative sources of renewable energy for cooking and heating in the urban areas. In the opinion of the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP), the project does not have a coherent plan on how to ensure that the benefits from the project are shared equitably with all beneficiaries, nor does it have a plan to engage the populations of the cities of Brazzaville and Pointe-Noire in the selection of species for reforestation. The TAP also considers that there is no viable measure for ensuring that renewable energy sources for cooking will be promoted in the urban areas. However, the use of fast-growing tree species could have adverse effects on biodiversity and climate change targets. The project should therefore ensure that the selection of species and functionality at the ecosystem level.
15. The project proponents are planning to develop a national plan to deliver an energy plan for the country. However, this plan will take some years to be delivered, and the transition to other sources of energy will not happen within the life of the project or in the years immediately following its completion. It is therefore important that both producers and consumers of wood understand the need to have a more sustainable use of forest resources for the long-term mitigation goals. The effects of climate change (CC) and land use change (LUC) involve this transition. In the coming decade, market preferences may change, and this could affect the project’s ability to deliver its intended benefits.
16. The project, which plans to invest over USD 46.5 million to achieve the expected reforestation and agroforestry targets, is not ambitious enough compared to other GCF forestry and land use projects in Africa. It also does not have a plan for how it will address the long-term mitigation goals are questionable, especially when the transition from wood fuel to other sources of energy will take some years to achieve. The impact potential is therefore medium.

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium-high

#### 1.2.1 Innovation
- It is argued that agroforestry and forestry systems are not a paradigm shift, as they represent common systems in many parts of the world. The project does not present any innovation in the possible approaches to natural or assisted regeneration or regenerative agriculture. It only refers to the use of different species for reforestation and agroforestry, which is simply a combination of different silviculture and agroforestry treatments.
- However, it is argued that the project could be considered as a paradigm shift in terms of the transfer of access and user rights on government land to smallholder farmers / producers with co-financing from the MEF. As this is a crucial aspect to ensure that farmers commit to promote sustainable landscapes. The project could also be considered as a paradigm shift if it were to use the available forest land from PROPAR and other land available on former state farms for beneficiaries for the implementation of agroforestry systems. However, the project does not mention either two modalities involve transfer of part of the land to beneficiaries for climate-resilient agroforestry or forestry activities in exchange for the project’s support to secure landowners’


---
**Page 101**
---

tenure reform and the shared remuneration system. Under this system, landowners commit to making land available to beneficiaries interested in implementing climate-smart agroforestry or forestry activities for a defined period of time (10 years) in exchange for shared remuneration payments that are shared equally.

24. To the TAP question as to what will happen, if after the 10 years landowners do not decide to renew the contracts because there is no money from the project to support their agricultural activities, the answer is that the project will be revised in terms of concept through improvements in livelihoods driven by sustainable and improved income generation in comparison with the baseline. The project will also ensure that the beneficiaries’ rights continue to contain lease renewal. However, this is a point of caution as the sustainability of the investment is not guaranteed.

25. The transfer of user rights will be subject to compliance to a list of specifications. These include the obligation for the beneficiaries to sustainably manage the transferred land following a pilot approach and the need to respect the tenure system in place, which is based on land rights the communities will care for their lands. However, this transfer should have strict conditions that are agreed upon by all parties.

26. In component 2, the targets of reforestation, agroforestry systems and natural regeneration seem to have been established without an appropriate landscape ecosystem analysis. The project proposes a set of five agroforestry systems and two forest-based proposals five agroforestry systems based on a set of criteria including increased carbon sequestration, improved soil fertility, water conservation, biodiversity, and income generation. These are: (i) cocoa–maize–groundnut; (ii) cocoa–plantain–groundnut–saffron; (iii) avocado–okra–aubergine; and (v) orange–make groundnut–pigeon pea–casava.

27. The project proposes the use of species such as Irvingia gabonensis and Acacia auriculiformis, which are well known in the Congo and widely used by local communities in Benoue National Park. However, there is no information on the distribution of these trees across the country as per national legislation and the CAR database. Acacia is one of the main fast- growing species widely valued and utilized by PRONAT as part of the government contribution to the national reforestation program.

28. However, as a recent BBC article citing experts from the Royal Botanic Gardens, Kew, points out, the selection of tree species for reforestation is not only about increasing biodiversity; the wrong tree in the wrong place can do more harm than good! In a biodiversity-rich country such as the Congo, the selection of tree species matters.

29. The project proposes that the production and consumption of livestock are intrinsically linked, as feedstock is collected mostly from clearing new land for cattle farming. However, the major threat to forest degradation is more related to the very low agricultural productivity in the country.

30. The country is willing to develop an energy master plan. As the country is rich in natural gas and coal, it would be useful to develop alternative energies such as solar; the project TGF should involve a phasing-out of wood as an energy alternative for cookstoves, and a shift to more sustainable options.

<sub>1 Law 20-180 of 13 June 2018 which sets the rules for land acquisition and sale. It is innovative in that it allows local communities to acquire land that they have been holding under customary rights and sell this land based on a land certificate.</sub>

<sub>3 PRONAT: National Reforestation Program. Available at <https://www.ibib.co/news/science-environment-55795816>. </sub>


---
**Page 102**
---

28. In component 3, the beneficiaries of climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry schemes are to be supported in developing and implementing bankable business models and market schemes. The project will establish partnerships with IFAD/FAO service providers tasked with assisting small farmers in developing (i) low-emission, climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry business plans; (ii) providing technical advisory services ranging from production to processing, including marketing advice; and (iii) financing approved business plans under a shared-cost-financing mechanism.

27. One of the main weaknesses of this project is the lack of analysis on the type of business models that are most likely to be successful in the market for climate-resilient crops produced by the different small farmers such as to develop resilient and marketable value chains. In addition, it is not clear whether the proposed agroforestry and forestry models need to be subsidized, in which case it is very unlikely that strong business models are foreseen.

28. The project proposes to link producers to the market by supporting the development and implementation of bankable business plans for small farmers. The main weaknesses of beneficiaries and selected off-takers / agro industrial enterprises in the Congo throughout a more equitable value chain include: (i) lack of access to finance; (ii) lack of quality climate products from beneficiaries' farms have been identified as: (i) company food enterprises; (ii) institutional off-takers; (iii) local farmers’ markets; and (iv) urban wholesalers and retailers operating in the Congo. The project proposes to link producers to these actors by providing names of specific companies/markets, the products they deal in, and their location. Letters of intent are provided for each actor.

29. However, the project needs to match the supply of (proposed agroforestry models) and the demand for the diversity of crops that the market is willing to buy. The project cannot expect to create a market for climate-resilient crops if it does not also aim at creating effective and equitable value chains, with increased profits to the communities. Moreover, the project will need to provide services without which quality control and consistent volumes have no negotiating power.

30. The project proposes to create strategic partnerships with national microcredit institutions, commercial banks, and private supply chain actors in climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry initiatives created by the beneficiaries. Several financial institutions with some interest in supporting small farmers in developing low-emission, climate-resilient crops proposed by the project were identified during project formulation. The project presents a list of six potential partners that are willing to provide technical assistance and/or financial support.

The project expects to mainstream green investment criteria specific to the climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry sector into existing national policies and strategies, in order to develop a financial inclusion strategy, and to develop inclusive financial products. However, in line with the above, it will be difficult to provide financial alternatives to enterprises and farms that have no access to finance or are not able to generate sufficient income to enable them to blend financial resources so as to ensure better financial conditions (interest rates, guarantees, longer repayment periods) for small farmers.

31. The TDC is structured on a very elementary linear basis, stating that, if the tenure access and the tenure rights are secured, the low-emission, climate-resilient agroforestry and forestry systems will be more productive, resilient to climate change impacts, carbon sequestration enhanced, then deforestation and forest degradation associated greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions will be reduced. The TDC is based on a linear relationship between CO2 and NOx. It has weak assumptions, does not present correlations between the different components, and does not address the need for an integrated energy plan to reduce deforestation.

### 1.2.3 Potential for knowledge and learning


---
**Page 103**
---

10. The project will support the transfer and adoption of low-carbon agroforestry and forestry systems to change current slash-and-burn practices. The project expects to establish farmer field schools to train beneficiaries in agroforestry and forestry practices with the technical assistance of the relevant national agencies, such as the National Institute for Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries. However, the real specificity around the proposed selection of species will be determined by the beneficiary farmers themselves through a farmer-to-farmer transfer scheme for PREFOREST which will depend mainly on site-specific demand and will be further defined during project implementation. The choice of species will also depend on the knowledge/skills of the beneficiaries for the establishment of such agroforestry systems.

13. Regarding learning, the project will develop demonstration and trial plots in order to guarantee the success of the proposed agroforestry systems. These trials will be carried out with the Institut National de Recherche Forestière, Institut National de Recherche Agronomique, and other relevant institutions such as the French Agricultural Research Centre for International Development.

2.3.3 Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment and regulatory framework

As noted above, in order to create an enabling environment, the project will need to have a strong economic rationale – one that links farmers with markets with a stronger economic and climate change analysis. If farmers end up planting any type of product just based on market conditions, it is likely that the environmental and social criteria for agroforestry systems will not be met, and it could end up not supporting the needed enabling environment.

In terms of the financial scheme, besides providing capacity building of financial partners and technical assistance to beneficiaries in the establishment of agroforestry systems, the project should revise the financial mechanisms available to enable agricultural credit at low interest rates and with longer repayment periods, and forge capacity-building.

2.4.2 Scalability and replicability

The project's major challenge is not on establishing agroforestry production systems, the viability of which will be dependent on subsidies. Scalability is therefore constrained due to the fact that, once GCF financing has ended, the replication potential will be severely affected by a lack of financial support. The establishment of agroforestry systems in large areas would be difficult to achieve, due to the already low budget allocation to the agriculture and forestry sectors.

The lack of proper market schemes and equitable value chains could be a real risk to the project's sustainability and to the possibility of scaling or replicating the project interventions.


1.3 Sustainable development potential

Scale: High


### Environmental co-benefits

20. The production systems promoted by the project will include nitrogen-fixing trees (e.g. Acacia spp.), which will significantly increase soil fertility, especially in the savannah areas where most of the farmers are currently engaged in slash-and-burn agriculture and rely heavily on only acacia species, which could transform the biodiversity and ecosystems. The project should provide a list of suitable tree species for each beneficiary farmer to plant in their fields, especially in savannah–forest transition areas affected by slash-and-burn agriculture and farmland forestation. The species should be selected to ensure the restoration of degraded soils and maintain soil fertility over time. The selection of nitrogen-fixing trees will therefore be a top priority for this project, and for every project. Merely planting acacia species as fast-growing


---
**Page 104**
---

species and nitrogen-fixing trees is not a good strategy to maximize biodiversity recovery, or guard against the impacts of climate change.

The project will establish forestry and agroforestry systems that will supply wood and food to selected communities, avoiding the expansion of the agricultural frontier and also allowing the restoration of strata forests in the buffer zone of the Mayone National Park on the border of Mayonoe. Likewise, it is a very rich and important ecosystem. It will also reduce the pressure on forests and other natural resources, which are currently being overexploited, thereby contributing to biodiversity conservation.

Some of these ecological services include the restoration of water, which will support the regeneration of riparian vegetation, control of floods, and reduce soil erosion. Moreover, if assisted restoration is done using a variety of species, it could increase biodiversity, support natural flood regulation, and improve water quality. Agroforestry systems can also be an important ecosystem services such as pollution and pest control, as well as the provision of food and non-food products.

### 1.3.4 Economic benefits

The project expects to create at least 11,200 decent direct and indirect green jobs through agroforestry and forestry systems, most of which are expected to persist even after the end of the project.

Improved and diversified agricultural production systems will ensure food security and provide opportunities to connect to markets, generating revenues to achieve a better quality of life for the communities. By diversifying crop production, the communities could be able to have more stable income throughout the year.

The restored forests could provide non-timber products for alternative medicines, building materials, and other uses.

Property rights will allow communities to acquire land and to support their livelihoods with forestry and agroforestry systems. Moreover, the contractors with landowners will benefit both through the sale of timber and the production of non-timber forest products, generating revenues for both parties. Having legalized contracts will allow them to negotiate better financial terms.

The project could support banking solutions to allow the economic activities to be more sustainable and prosperous over time. Individuals who have land titles will be able to access credit for their enterprises, which would help producers with livelihoods to deal with loans as they will have other sources of income.

### 1.3.7 Social co-benefits

Households could ensure more food security with the implementation of agroforestry systems, and have a better standard of living from increased income. Property rights will allow communities to acquire land and support their livelihoods with forestry and agroforestry systems, which would provide benefits to their future livelihoods.

Communities will also gain knowledge of climate change, and will be able to decide on their productive systems and on the conservation of natural resources. Once forestry and agroforestry systems have been restored, the effects in terms of microclimates will benefit the communities by reducing heat stress during the dry season.

The project will facilitate access to markets and financial schemes, allowing communities to force to increase their income and generate an increase in user income generation. This project will also support the involvement of women in developing food systems and in improving their ability to support their households.


---
**Page 105**
---

1. **Gender-sensitive development**

   * In 2015, women represent 70 per cent of the labour force and are responsible for 68-80 per cent of the country's subsistence crop production. According to the Constitution of the Republic of the Congo (2015), women have the same rights as men. The country has a national gender policy that aims at the economic, social, cultural, political, and legal equality and political empowerment of women and girls in the Congo, driven by the Ministry for the Promotion of Women and Girls.
   * The project carried out a gender assessment to identify any gender disparities that could hinder full participation by women and their enjoyment of the benefits of the project. Based on a solid analysis of the gender dimension of the strategy of the project that serves as a basis for the development of the gender action plan.

51. The project includes detailed activities, targets, and actions to mainstream gender-sensitive approaches in all aspects of the project. These include women's empowerment by building their capacities for effective management of the value chains of the climate-smart agricultural technologies (CSAT) that they will be trained on. The project expects to directly benefit about 14,800 women (and indirectly benefit 367,927 women).
   * Women will have access to land ownership, market schemes and bank credit, which all improve their economic status.
52. Moreover, the agroforestry systems will allow the woman to feed their families with more diversified produce, and so acquire revenue to pay for other basic needs such as health and education.

53. The project will work in close collaboration with the Ministry for the Promotion of Women and Girls (MFPF) to ensure a strengthened ownership of interventions related to gender, as well as project sustainability.


1.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

1.4.1 Vulnerability of the country and vulnerable groups

54. The Congo's economic growth has remained on track despite a weak domestic environment. The growth upturn in 2018 (1.6 per cent) accelerated slightly to 2019 to 2.2 per cent, attributable to the oil sector, which grew 5.5 per cent, and construction and public works, up by 37.4 per cent. However, the country's economic growth is still fragile as it relies heavily on oil for 35 per cent of gross domestic product, 85 per cent of exports, and 80 per cent of tax revenues.
   * The Congo has major agricultural potential as well as enormous natural resources not yet fully exploited (oil, iron, lead, zinc, potash, copper, uranium, diamonds, phosphates, manganese, gold, and timber). However, the country's economy is still heavily dependent on favourable market conditions, with oil prices holding steady in late 2018 and the resumption of demand for minerals. The economic outlook remains fragile as the country's economy continues to decline, contracting by 5.5 per cent as a result of the weakening of activity in construction and public works.

56. More than half of the population is concentrated in the country's two largest cities, Brazzaville and Pointe-Noire. The rest of the country is one of the least densely populated areas


---
**Page 106**
---

In Africa, just 12.8 people per square kilometre. The proportion of the population living below the poverty line fell from 51 in 2005 to 41 per cent in 2011. However, the extreme poverty rate appears to have increased since 2016, especially in rural areas, as a result of the decline in agricultural production and the increase in food prices. In urban areas in the south of the country. Fewer than 4.9 per cent of them are covered by the country's social protection system. In rural areas, where most people live, they have no access to formal areas poor people are either unemployed, or they are dependent on subsistence farming as their main source of income.

The country’s human capital index stands at 0.42, which is below the average for middle-income countries. Maternal and infant mortality rates remain high, with 5 per cent of children not reaching age five in the country.

In terms of institutional capacity, the proposed points put on concern around the budget given by the central government to important institutions. For example, the agricultural support fund of the SNR received a budget of 10 billion CFA francs for the period from January 2008 to 2007 not allowing it to carry out its mission. Moreover, the SNR, which is responsible for the management of the country’s forests and wildlife, has been unable to implement tasks necessary human and budgetary resources. In view of the TAP, this represents either a lack of country ownership or a lack of country priority to support agriculture forestry and rural livelihoods.

In terms of climate change, the country has one of the lowest deforestation rates in the Congo Basin. However, the country is still vulnerable to climate change. The largest share of GHG emissions from land use, land-use change and forestry have represented the largest share of total emissions for more than a decade estimated at about 40 MtCO2eq/year in 2016.

Annual average temperature has increased by 0.5°C since 1973, with an increase of 0.8°C since 1996. Climate projections indicate that the country will remain uncharted over the next few decades. Climate projections from the Representative Concentration Pathway (RCP) 4.5 emissions scenario show a temperature increase of between 2 and 3.5°C by 2050, with important regional variations. Precipitation is projected to remain relatively stable but with a decrease between 1976-2005 and 2080-2099. Decrease in precipitation will be more pronounced in the north where growth will be lower, and less pronounced at least on 30 mm by 2030-2050.

The country’s agriculture sector is one of the most vulnerable sectors directly affected by climate change. The agriculture sector is very underdeveloped and employs about 61 million people. Contribution to the country's gross domestic product is stagnant at about 4 per cent, which is essential for economic development. The country is located in the Congo, and therefore highly dependent on the climate. Agriculture, including in the project area, is based almost entirely on rainfed farming. In the project area, only a small proportion of land (10 per cent) is used to grow food crops. Subsistence farming represents 80 per cent of cultivated land, estimated at 200,000 ha out of 10 millions ha of fertile land. The impacts of climate change will increase the


---
**Page 107**
---

risk of food insecurity risk in a country that is already 75 per cent dependent on food imports to meet the needs of its population.

64. Under the projected climate changes, the climate models of the IFAD Climate Adaptation in Rural Development Assessment Tool indicate significant production decreases for major crops over most regions. For example, under an emission scenario that leads to global warming of about 4°C by 2100, cassava production is predicted to decrease from a current level of around 35 million tonnes per year to less than 18 million tonnes per cent at the national level.

65. In summary, the country is rich in terms of natural resources but with very poor socioeconomic conditions, especially in the rural sector, and weak institutional capacity to support smallholders' livelihoods and forestry and agroforestry systems.

### 1.5 Country ownership

#### Scale: High

##### 1.5.1 Alignment with national climate strategy

66. Since 2008, the Congo has been involved in the REDD-plus process in order to: (i) contribute to combating climate change; (ii) conserve forest ecosystems; (iii) alleviate poverty; and (iv) support sustainable development. The country is committed to a national strategy towards emergence in 2015. The REDD-plus national strategy developed within the context of this process has been aligned with the country's National Strategy for Sustainable Development, its MRV-plus process has been supported by the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility and CAFI.

67. The Congo has ratified the Paris Agreement and submitted its NDC to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). The country is committed to low-carbon development policy and support for the global effort to reduce gas emissions. Under the low-carbon scenario, the Congo aims to achieve a reduction of 10 per cent by 2025, but only 54 per cent (19 Mio tCO2e) in 2030. To achieve these emission reductions under this low-carbon scenario, the Congo is seeking, among others, to: (i) reduce unplanned deforestation to below 20 per cent of total forest cover by 2025; (ii) increase the share of protected areas from 16 per cent in 2013 to 40 per cent in 2025; (iii) generalize the use of improved cookstoves (20 per cent in 2025, and 50 per cent in 2030); and (iv) improve energy efficiency by 6.7 per cent in 2013 to 18.4 per cent in 2025.

68. These targets are fully aligned with the ambitions of PROFOREST.

69. The Congo adopted a forestry law on November 2000 and a forest code in a legal framework that includes land-use planning, community development, and participation, based on rational planning and participatory resources management.

70. Furthermore, the project supports the country's land-planning efforts, in particular Decree no. 2013-280 of 25 June 2013, related to the creation of community management and development plans for forest lands and the promotion of sustainable forest management through community development based on a community land-use plan. Moreover, Law no. 21-2018 of 13 July 2018 provides that it is possible for local communities to register land that they hold by custom and to develop this land through participatory planning.

71. Both land-use rights and forest management are key components to achieving the country's NDC commitments and the REDD-plus national strategy.

#### Capacity of accredited entities and executing entities to deliver

72. FAO will serve as the accredited entity (AE) and as executing entity (EE) for the project. As such, FAO will be responsible for the overall management of this project. FAO is the agency in


---
**Page 108**
---

The Green Climate Fund

### The Funder System that is Specifically Involved in Agriculture and Forestry Issues, and it is also Accredited by GCF: The Organization has a Solid Reputation in the Fields of Forestry, Agriculture and Food, with a Long History of Climate-Related Knowledge and Innovation, in Particular in the Context of Sustainable Land Management

72. The AE and EE functions of FAD will be well separated internally to differentiate the project coordination and the project supervision functions. As an IEL, FAD will take responsibility for the overall management of the project, including all its components through a dedicated team. FAD will oversee coordination of planning and implementation of project activities with the Ministry of Forest Economy (MOFE) and other partners of the Congo, CAFI and IFAD. CAFI and IFAD will, according to their rules and regulations (which are fully compatible), provide co-financing for the project. The IEL will have a clear advantage in executing the co-financed activities. FAD will establish a project management unit (PMU) to handle the coordination and implementation of the project (day-to-day management).

73. It is important to note that the PMU will be dissolved at the end of the project after project completion, with all the need to ensure capacity-building of national institutions. The PMU will be led by a technical coordinator (TC), who will be supported by his/her chief technical advisor (CTA) – an IEL staff member earning competitive international rates – who will lead the project oversight. Although the CTA will work together with the national coordinator for the project, he/she is not responsible to him/her and will report directly to the PMU. This arrangement will be implemented only during the early years of the project, subsequently transferring the responsibility to the MOFE.

74. The Ministry of Agriculture will act as a co-EE of PREFREST and will execute activities co-financed by IFAD. The activities co-financed by CAFI will be executed by a partner to be pre-selected in accordance with the terms of reference (ToR) for the selection process, subject to an arrangement for working closely with FAD. For CAFI and IFAD co-financing, the governance arrangements will be established through a joint agreement between the two parties aligned with the obligations of IEL under the accreditation master agreement and with FAD regulations.

75. The project will have a steering committee co-chaired by the Ministry of Forest Economy, with representation from the Ministries of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries, Land-use Planning, Environment, Water Resources, Energy and Hydrocarbons, Tourism and Technology Innovation, Energy and Hydraulics, and Tourism and the Environment. It will also involve the CAFI/IFAD Joint Technical Committee (JTC), the CAFI Country Director, the IFAD PA/IE focal point, and CAFI focal point. Moreover, it will have a technical committee composed of representatives from the Ministry of Forest Economy.

76. The Congo received support from CAFI in developing its REDD+ plus investment plan, which highlights the Government’s priority in implementing its REDD-plus national strategy, and aligns investments with the country’s development priorities as defined by the Government in its REDD-plus national strategy for the period 2010-2025. CAFI supported the preparation of the Congo’s REDD+ plus investment plan, which is a key component of this investment plan, and because it will support its deployment in the southern part of the country, which currently receives no funding for the deployment of REDD-plus investments.

77. CAFI has also provided technical assistance to the Congo in developing a project in New York as a collaborative partnership between a coalition of willing donors (France, Germany, Norway, the United States and Switzerland) and the Congo. The project is being developed with the support of the Central African Republic, the Congo, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, Equatorial Guinea and Gabon), and Brazil in a South-South partner. The goals of CAFI are to “recognize and preserve forest ecosystems that are under threat from deforestation and degradation and contribute to sustainable development.”

## Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders

### Specific arrangements were made to guarantee effective participation by women and indigenous peoples.

In total, 415 individuals, including 62 women, were consulted. In addition, a framework was developed for the engagement of women and indigenous peoples into participatory forest management processes in accordance with the principles of forestry systems promoted by the FAD. The FAD also identified the systems best adapted to the local context and the specific needs of these groups.

### Some new consultations with local communities were held between November and December 2020 in order to improve the content of the project document. During these consultations, the communities expressed their interest in participating in participatory forest management processes promoted by the FAD and identified systems best adapted to the local context and the specific needs of women and indigenous peoples.

However, during the consultation processes and in the reports presented to the Secretariat, only the above general items were addressed with the communities. The project will need to be further developed to ensure that all relevant stakeholders have a better understanding who will be involved, as well as the individual and aggregated commitments.
